Institute of Applied Mathematics and Statistics, University of Hohenheim, Schloss Hohenheim 1, 70599 Stuttgart, Germanyjonas.jansen@uni-hohenheim.de Slow-moving pattern interfaces in general directions for a two-dimensional Swift–Hohenberg-type equation Jonas Jansen Abstract We rigorously prove the bifurcation of slow-moving pattern interfaces with general direction in a two-dimensional Swift–Hohenberg-type model close to a Turing instability for a large class of nonlinearities. These interfaces describe the invasion of stripe and hexagonal patterns into the spatially homogeneous state and model a possible mechanism for pattern formation, as observed in a wide range of real-world applications. For this, we develop a rigorous framework to establish the existence of such solutions using spatial dynamics and non-standard centre manifold theory. Our approach exploits geometric and algebraic structures generic to $\mathrm{O}(2)$ -symmetric pattern-forming systems near a Turing instability, and addresses fundamental technical challenges due to a non-uniform spectral gap around the imaginary axis, quadratic resonances induced by the hexagonal structure, and the high-dimensional phase space of the reduced equations. Contents 1  Introduction The formation of spatially regular structures is ubiquitous in natural systems and can be observed in hydrodynamic convection problems [ Pea58 , SH77 ] , vegetation systems [ Kla99 , Mer18 ] , biological and chemical systems [ GM72 , OS91 ] , and engineering applications [ CB95 , Ban+19 ] . These spatial patterns typically arise when a spatially homogeneous state destabilises in response to a change in a system parameter. One of the most famous examples is the Turing instability [ Tur52 ] , where the spatially homogeneous state becomes unstable in a neighbourhood of a finite Fourier wave number, which leads to the formation of steady, spatially periodic structures such as hexagonal and stripe patterns. In applications, these spatial patterns typically arise in the wake of an invasion front, which facilitates the spatial transition from the pattern to the unstable homogeneous state, see Figure 1 . These fronts can appear as radial invasion phenomena, in which the pattern spreads into the unstable state from an initial point defect [ Bod+91 ] , or as planar invasion fronts, in which the pattern and the unstable state are separated by a moving planar interface [ NT95 ] . a Pattern interface for angle $\theta=0$ . b Pattern interface for angle $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Figure 1 : Example of two-dimensional planar pattern interfaces showing the invasion of a hexagonal pattern into the spatially homogeneous steady state for different directions. In this paper, we study the existence of two-dimensional, planar pattern interfaces close to a Turing instability. It is well known that the dynamics close to these instabilities are universal, and the emerging structures are independent of the specific application, cf. Section 1.1 . Therefore, we analyse the existence of pattern interfaces in a semilinear Swift–Hohenberg-type equation $\partial_{t}u=L(\nabla;\mu)u+N(u,Du,D^{2}u,D^{3}u;\vartheta),\qquad L(\nabla;\mu)u=-(1+\Delta)^{2}u+\mu u$ (1.1) with $u(t,\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}$ , a set of parameters $\vartheta\in\Theta$ , time $t\geqslant 0$ and space $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ . The classical Swift–Hohenberg equation with $N(u)=-u^{3}$ was first proposed as a model for the formation of thermally-driven convection cells in fluid applications [ SH77 ] . The equation ( 1.1 ) undergoes a Turing instability at $\mu=0$ , that is, there exists a critical absolute wave number $k_{c}=1$ , such that, for $\mu>0$ , solutions to the linearised equation of the form $e^{\lambda t-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}$ grow exponentially for $|\mathbf{k}|$ close to $k_{c}$ and decay otherwise, cf. Appendix A . Therefore, this is a prototypical model to study the dynamics close to such an instability. Planar pattern interfaces for a similar model with $N(u)=-\beta|\nabla u|^{2}-u^{3}$ were first constructed in [ Doe+03 ] , where the authors obtained solutions travelling in $x_{1}$ -direction under the assumption that $|\beta|\ll 1$ . Here, we develop a fully rigorous framework for two-dimensional pattern interfaces connecting periodic solutions on a hexagonal Fourier lattice and travelling in general directions $\mathbf{d}=(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))$ satisfying $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ for a Swift–Hohenberg-type equation with a generic class of nonlinearities. The framework relies on a spatial-dynamics approach with a non-standard normal-form transformation and centre manifold reduction with a non-uniform spectral gap. Specifically, we address two fundamentally new mathematical obstacles: quadratic resonances inherent to the hexagonal lattice structure induce singular scalings in the normal-form analysis, and the insufficient gain of regularity in the front direction, which requires a functional-analytic framework using mixed-regularity Sobolev spaces. This approach reduces the construction of pattern interfaces to proving the existence of travelling fronts in a system of degenerate Ginzburg–Landau equations. Here, we prove the existence of selected heteroclinic orbits, which completes the fully rigorous construction of specific planar fronts. We refer to Section 1.4 for a detailed list of the main results. While the specific analysis is restricted to ( 1.1 ), our framework applies much more broadly to generic rotational- and reflectional-invariant, semilinear pattern-forming systems close to a Turing instability. Indeed, wherever possible, we exploit generic geometric and algebraic properties of such systems in our analysis rather than relying on explicit computations. 1.1  Formal amplitude system and nonlinear waves As mentioned above, the dynamics of small-amplitude solutions close to a Turing instability are universal, that is, the dynamics are governed by a system of simpler PDEs whose form is independent of the specific application. To make this precise, we introduce the Fourier lattice $\Gamma$ given by $\Gamma:=\Bigl\{\sum_{j=1}^{3}n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}:n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\Bigr\},$ (1.2) which is spanned by $\mathbf{k}_{j}$ , $j=1,2,3$ , defined by $\mathbf{k}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{k}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
\sqrt{3}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{k}_{3}=-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\sqrt{3}\end{pmatrix}.$ (1.3) Functions, which have a Fourier series with modes in $\Gamma$ , are periodic with respect to a hexagonal lattice and, in particular, contain stripe and hexagonal patterns. Close to the onset of instability, i.e. for $\mu=\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}$ with $0<\varepsilon\ll 1$ and $\mu_{0}>0$ , solutions to ( 1.1 ) are, to leading order, concentrated at critical Fourier modes $\Gamma_{0}:=\Gamma\cap\{|\boldsymbol{\gamma}|=1\}=\{\pm\mathbf{k}_{j}\,:\,j=1,2,3\}$ , where the solutions to the linear equation grow exponentially. Therefore, they are of the form $u(t,\mathbf{x})=\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{3}A_{j}(\varepsilon^{2}t,\varepsilon\mathbf{x})e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}}+c.c.+h.o.t.,$ (1.4) where $c.c.$ and $h.o.t.$ denote complex-conjugated and higher-order terms, respectively. The amplitude modulations $A_{j}(T,\mathbf{X})\in\mathbb{C}$ depend on the slow temporal variable $T=\varepsilon^{2}t$ and the slow spatial variable $\mathbf{X}=\varepsilon\mathbf{x}$ . To formulate the evolution equations for the amplitude modulations, we make additional assumptions on the nonlinearity. Assumption A. Assume that the nonlinearity is rotation-invariant and reflection-invariant, in the sense that, if $u$ is a solution to ( 1.1 ), then also $u\circ R$ is a solution for any $R\in\mathrm{O}(2)$ . Furthermore, we split $N(u,Du,D^{2}u,D^{3}u;\vartheta)=N_{2}(u,u;\vartheta)+N_{3}(u,u,u;\vartheta)+R(u;\vartheta)$ with a bilinear form $N_{2}$ , a trilinear form $N_{3}$ and a polynomial remainder $R$ , which contains all terms of degree at least four. Since the hexagonal lattice is resonant, i.e. $\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{3}=0$ , quadratic terms of leading-order modes can generate large contributions at critical modes. To control these interactions, we need to assume that they gain an additional order of $\varepsilon$ through the nonlinearity. Assumption B. Assume that there exists a submanifold $\Theta_{0}$ of the parameter space $\Theta$ such that $N_{2}(e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{x}};\vartheta)=0$ for all $|\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell}|=1$ and $\vartheta\in\Theta_{0}$ . Furthermore, we assume that there are curves $\varepsilon\mapsto\vartheta=\vartheta(\varepsilon)$ in a neighbourhood of $\Theta_{0}$ such that $N_{2}(e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{x}};\vartheta)=\varepsilon\beta_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ for all $|\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell}|=1$ . Remark 1.1. We point out that this is a weaker assumption compared to the construction of [ Doe+03 ] , where $N_{2}(u)=-\beta|\nabla u|^{2}$ and $\beta=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ is used. Remark 1.2. We note that both Assumptions A and B hold true in relevant physical examples such as asymptotic thin-film models of the Bénard–Marangoni problem, cf. [ HJ25 ] . Under Assumptions A and B , the amplitude modulations $A_{j}$ satisfy the Ginzburg–Landau system $\begin{split}\partial_{T}A_{1}&=4(\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{X}})^{2}A_{1}+\mu_{0}A_{1}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1},\\
\partial_{T}A_{2}&=4(\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{X}})^{2}A_{2}+\mu_{0}A_{2}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2},\\
\partial_{T}A_{3}&=4(\mathbf{k}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{X}})^{2}A_{3}+\mu_{0}A_{3}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3}\\
\end{split}$ (1.5) with coefficients $\beta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}$ and $K_{0},K_{2}\in\mathbb{R}$ . We point out that the coefficients are the same in all three equations due to the rotational invariance and they are real-valued due to the reflectional invariance, cf. [ Hoy07 , Sec. 5.4] . The system ( 1.5 ) can formally be derived from ( 1.1 ), or, in fact, a general $\mathrm{O}(n)$ -symmetric pattern-forming system close to a Turing instability, through multiscale expansion. In spatially one-dimensional systems, or more generally, systems on an infinite cylinder, there is a well-developed justification theory, which shows that the resulting amplitude equation, the classical real Ginzburg–Landau equation, makes good predictions for the dynamics over a long time interval. Moreover, there are approximation results stating that any small solution will, to leading order, eventually be localised in Fourier space at the critical wave numbers, and the corresponding amplitudes are again determined by the one-dimensional amplitude equation. It is expected that these results directly extend to the two-dimensional case if the solution is sufficiently localised in Fourier space at the lattice points in $\Gamma$ and the nonlinearity satisfies the smallness condition in Assumption B . We refer to [ SU17 , Ch. 10] for a more detailed discussion of this topic. As discussed above, the main goal of this paper is to show the existence of pattern interfaces moving in direction $\mathbf{d}\in S^{1}$ . These solutions correspond to travelling-wave solutions in the Ginzburg–Landau system ( 1.5 ) of the form $A_{j}(T,X)=A_{j}(\Xi)$ with $\Xi=\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{X}-c_{0}T$ and $c_{0}>0$ . These waves are solutions to the system $\begin{split}0&=4(\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{d})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{1}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}+\mu_{0}A_{1}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1},\\
0&=4(\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{d})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{2}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}+\mu_{0}A_{2}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2},\\
0&=4(\mathbf{k}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{d})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{3}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}+\mu_{0}A_{3}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3}.\end{split}$ (1.6) We highlight that the dynamics of the system ( 1.6 ) depend on the chosen direction since the diffusion coefficients explicitly depend on it. Moreover, this shows that there is a set of special directions $\mathbf{d}$ which satisfy $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}=0$ for some $j=1,2,3$ . In this case, the travelling-wave system ( 1.6 ) has a degenerate-elliptic structure. Such travelling waves with finite speed $c_{0}$ correspond to slow-moving pattern interfaces in the Swift–Hohenberg equation ( 1.1 ) moving with speed $\varepsilon c_{0}$ in direction $\mathbf{d}$ . Indeed, we find that $\Xi=\varepsilon(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\varepsilon c_{0}t)$ . These fronts are the relevant class of solutions for describing invasion processes in real-world systems, in the sense that, heuristically, front speeds of order $\varepsilon$ are selected, cf. Section 1.2 . Moreover, the direction-dependence is a distinct feature of this class of solutions. In particular, the construction of fast-moving fronts, considered for example in [ CE86 , HJ25 ] , is independent of the direction. This can also be seen by considering the formal limit $c_{0}\to\infty$ in ( 1.5 ), which can be made rigorous using fast-slow methods, cf. Section 4.4 . 1.2  Selected speeds and marginal stability analysis As is typical for invasion phenomena into unstable states, our analysis yields a continuous family of fronts with different speeds for each direction. This raises the natural question: which front is selected, i.e., which front forms in the time-evolution problem starting from steep or step-like initial data? This question has attracted much attention recently, and for invading fronts in spatially one-dimensional reaction-diffusion equations where the spatially homogeneous invading state is exponentially stable, a robust framework for stability and front selection is available, see e.g. [ AS22 , AHS25 ] . In addition, there are recent advances in generalising this framework to steady pattern-forming fronts where the front is stationary in an appropriate co-moving frame, see [ ACR26 ] . In contrast, the pattern interfaces constructed in this paper are non-steady as they describe a connection of two stationary patterns via a moving front. To the best of our knowledge, a rigorous framework is not available for such non-steady pattern-forming fronts, even in one spatial dimension, or spatially two-dimensional reaction-diffusion systems. Although a rigorous framework is lacking, there are formal conjectures about front selection in situations where the front dynamics are determined by the linear behaviour about the unstable state, which are referred to as pulled fronts. Here, the conjectured selected front speed can be determined through the marginal stability conjecture , see e.g. [ vSaa03 ] and [ AHS25 , Def. 6.8 and Conj. 6.9] . For this, one observes that the unstable state, when viewed in a moving reference frame, transitions from absolutely unstable to convectively unstable as the reference frame speed increases. That is, at low speeds, a perturbation of the unstable state will grow exponentially at each fixed point, while at high speeds it will decay exponentially at each fixed point. The marginal stability conjecture states that the selected speed is the one at which the system transitions from absolute to convective instability. In practice, the conjectured selected speed is determined from the dispersion relation of the linearisation about the unstable state in a co-moving frame $\boldsymbol{\xi}=\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{d}ct$ , which is obtained by making an ansatz of the form $u(t,\boldsymbol{\xi})=e^{\lambda t+\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}}$ with $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ and $\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}$ . In our case, the resulting dispersion relation reads as $d(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\nu},c):=L(\boldsymbol{\nu};\mu)+c\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}-\lambda=-(1+\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})^{2}+\mu+c\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}-\lambda.$ (1.7) Following [ HS14 ] , we now assume periodicity in the transverse direction of the front, i.e., in $\mathbf{d}^{\perp}$ -direction. This is reflected by writing $\boldsymbol{\nu}=\nu\mathbf{d}+ik_{\perp}\mathbf{d}^{\perp}$ with $\nu\in\mathbb{C}$ , $k_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}$ . In addition, since $\lambda$ is the growth rate of the linear problem in a moving frame with speed $c$ , to determine the selected speed, we set $\lambda=i\omega$ with $\omega\in\mathbb{R}$ . Then, we obtain the spatial wave number $\nu\in\mathbb{C}$ , the speed $c>0$ and the temporal oscillation $\omega\in\mathbb{R}$ by assuming that $\nu\mapsto d(i\omega,\nu\mathbf{d}+ik_{\perp}\mathbf{d}^{\perp},c)$ has a pinched double root, cf. [ AHS25 , Def. 2.3] . A necessary condition for this is $d(i\omega,\nu\mathbf{d}+ik_{\perp}\mathbf{d}^{\perp},c)=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\nu}d(i\omega,\nu\mathbf{d}+ik_{\perp}\mathbf{d}^{\perp},c)=0$ for fixed $k_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}$ . While this is not a sufficient condition for a pinched double root, it is often used in practice to determine the critical speed. Applied to our case, where the dispersion relation is given by ( 1.7 ), we first determine $\operatorname{Re}\nu$ and $\operatorname{Im}\nu$ by solving $\begin{split}\operatorname{Im}(\partial_{\nu}L(\nu\mathbf{d}+ik_{\perp}\mathbf{d}^{\perp},\mu))&=0,\\
\operatorname{Re}(L(\nu\mathbf{d}+ik_{\perp}\mathbf{d}^{\perp},\mu))&=c\operatorname{Re}\nu=\operatorname{Re}(\partial_{\nu}L(\nu\mathbf{d}+ik_{\perp}\mathbf{d}^{\perp},\mu))\operatorname{Re}\nu,\end{split}$ cf. [ AHS25 , Eq. (2.14)] . Since we are looking for fronts which invade the unstable state and travel from left to right, we look for solutions to the linear equation which decay as $\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}\to\infty$ . Therefore, assuming $\operatorname{Re}(\nu)<0$ , we find $\begin{split}\nu&=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{3}}\sqrt{\sqrt{\left(1-k^{2}_{\perp}\right)^{2}+6\mu}-(1-k_{\perp}^{2})}\pm i\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\sqrt{\left(1-k^{2}_{\perp}\right)^{2}+6\mu}+3(1-k^{2}_{\perp})},\\
c&=\frac{4}{3\sqrt{3}}\left(\sqrt{\left(1-k^{2}_{\perp}\right)^{2}+6\mu}+2(1-k^{2}_{\perp})\right)\sqrt{\sqrt{\left(1-k^{2}_{\perp}\right)^{2}+6\mu}-(1-k^{2}_{\perp})},\\
\omega&=\mp\frac{1}{6\sqrt{3}}\left(5\sqrt{\left(1-k^{2}_{\perp}\right)^{2}+6\mu}+7(1-k^{2}_{\perp})\right)\sqrt{\sqrt{\left(1-k^{2}_{\perp}\right)^{2}+6\mu}+3(1-k^{2}_{\perp})}\sqrt{\sqrt{\left(1-k^{2}_{\perp}\right)^{2}+6\mu}-(1-k^{2}_{\perp})}.\end{split}$ Assuming that $\mu=\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}$ , i.e., that the problem is close to the onset of instability, we find, by expanding with respect to $\varepsilon$ , that $\begin{split}\nu&=\pm i\sqrt{1-k_{\perp}^{2}}+\varepsilon\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{0}}{1-k_{\perp}^{2}}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),\\
c&=\varepsilon 4\sqrt{(1-k_{\perp}^{2})\mu_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),\\
\omega&=\mp\varepsilon 4(1-k_{\perp}^{2})\sqrt{\mu_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).\end{split}$ (1.8) From these calculations, we obtain two crucial observations. First, we note that the selected speed is of order $\varepsilon$ and therefore, slow-moving fronts are selected close to the onset of instability, cf. [ CE02 , Ave25 ] . Second, as already pointed out in [ HS14 ] , we find that the maximal speed is obtained at $k_{\perp}=0$ . Therefore, it is conjectured in [ HS14 ] that at the leading edge one finds stripe patterns orthogonal to the direction of the planar front, which seems to be confirmed by numerical experiments, see e.g. [ PN94 , CM99 ] . For a more detailed discussion, we refer to [ HS14 , AHS25 ] and Section 6 . 1.3  General strategies and main challenges To construct the pattern interfaces, we use a spatial-dynamics approach, [ Kir82 ] , where an elliptic system is written as a dynamical system by interpreting a spatial variable as ’time’. This approach has been used successfully to construct, primarily one-dimensional, pattern interfaces [ EW91 , HS99 , Doe+03 , FH15 , Hil20 , Hil22 , HJ25 ] . For this, we make the ansatz $u(t,\mathbf{x})=U(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\varepsilon c_{0}t,\mathbf{x})$ (1.9) and assume that $U(\xi,\mathbf{p})$ is periodic in $\mathbf{p}$ with respect to a hexagonal lattice, and that $U$ connects two different planar pattern solutions $u_{1}$ and $u_{2}$ (including the trivial one) to ( 1.1 ), that is, $\lim_{\xi\to-\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=u_{1}(\mathbf{p})\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\xi\to\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=u_{2}(\mathbf{p}).$ Inserting the ansatz ( 1.9 ) into ( 1.1 ) yields $-c\partial_{\xi}U=\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+\nabla_{\mathbf{p}};\mu,\vartheta)(U)$ for some smooth function $\mathcal{F}(\cdot;\mu,\vartheta)$ , where we used that $\nabla u=(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})U=\mathbf{d}(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})U+\mathbf{d}^{\perp}(\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})U$ . Since the nonlinearity $N$ in ( 1.1 ) contains at most third-order derivatives, the highest-order derivative in $\xi$ comes from the linear part and we can write $(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})^{4}U=c\partial_{\xi}U+(\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+\nabla_{\mathbf{p}};\mu,\vartheta)(U)+(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})^{4}U)$ (1.10) to isolate the $\partial_{\xi}^{4}U$ -term. Due to the periodicity in $\mathbf{p}$ , we can write $U$ as a Fourier series in $\mathbf{p}$ , that is $U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma}\hat{U}(\xi,\boldsymbol{\gamma})e^{i\boldsymbol{\gamma}\cdot\mathbf{p}}.$ Inserting this, we can write the resulting systems as a first-order system in $\xi$ , which reads as $\partial_{\xi}\hat{W}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\hat{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\mu,c)\hat{W}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}\hat{W}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\hat{\mathcal{N}}(\hat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma};\vartheta)$ (1.11) for all $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ , where $\hat{W}(\xi,\boldsymbol{\gamma})=(\hat{U}(\xi,\boldsymbol{\gamma}),(\partial_{\xi}+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})\hat{U}(\xi,\boldsymbol{\gamma}),(\partial_{\xi}+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{2}\hat{U}(\xi,\boldsymbol{\gamma}),(\partial_{\xi}+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{3}\hat{U}(\xi,\boldsymbol{\gamma}))$ and $\hat{\mathcal{L}}(\mu,c)=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma}\hat{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma};\mu,c)$ explicitly given in ( 3.22 ).
The nonlinearity $\mathcal{N}$ is given by $\hat{\mathcal{N}}(\hat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma};\vartheta)=(0,0,0,\hat{N}(\hat{W};\boldsymbol{\gamma};\vartheta))^{T},$ where $\hat{N}(\hat{W};\cdot;\vartheta)$ is the Fourier transform of $N(U,(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+\nabla_{p})U,(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+\nabla_{p})^{2}U,(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+\nabla_{p})^{3}U)$ with respect to $\mathbf{p}$ . We specifically note that $\hat{\mathcal{L}}$ and $\hat{\mathcal{N}}$ in ( 1.11 ) only contain Fourier multipliers of the form $(\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ which correspond to transverse derivatives. This will be crucial in our analysis as discussed in Section 3.1 . As in Section 1.1 , we introduce a small scaling parameter $\varepsilon>0$ by $\mu=\mu_{0}\varepsilon^{2}$ and $c=c_{0}\varepsilon$ with $\mu_{0},c_{0}=\mathcal{O}(1)$ . As discussed in Section 1.2 , this scaling mimics the expected spreading speed of a front evolving from compactly supported initial data close to the onset of instability, see also [ CE02 , Ave+25 ] and Appendix B . Using this rescaling, we introduce the following notation for the linear operators $L^{\varepsilon}(\nabla):=L(\nabla;\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}:=\hat{\mathcal{L}}(\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0},\varepsilon c_{0}).$ The next step is to reduce the infinite-dimensional dynamical system ( 1.11 ) to a finite-dimensional system using centre manifold theory. For this, we need to analyse the spectrum of the full linear operator $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}:=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})\quad\text{with}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}):=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}I.$ The main challenge in the centre-manifold analysis is that $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}$ has infinitely many, purely imaginary eigenvalues, which move away from the imaginary axis at different speeds for $\varepsilon>0$ , creating an $\varepsilon$ -dependent splitting between finitely many more-central and infinitely many less-central eigenvalues. Although a similar problem appeared in previous papers, see e.g. [ HS99 ] , in two-dimensional systems with a general class of quadratic nonlinearities, additional obstructions arise due to the resonance of the hexagonal lattice. In addition, the spectrum depends crucially on the direction $\mathbf{d}$ . Writing $\mathbf{d}=(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))$ , it turns out that the hyperbolic eigenvalues satisfy a spectral gap condition only under the condition that $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ and, to avoid further technicalities related to small-devisor problems, see Section 6 for a discussion, we adopt this assumption throughout the paper. Note that this condition is satisfied for $\theta=0$ and $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . The final step of the analysis is the derivation and analysis of the system of reduced equations on the centre manifold. As expected, this system is indeed given by the travelling-wave system ( 1.6 ) obtained from the amplitude system ( 1.5 ). Due to the high dimensionality of the reduced system, constructing heteroclinic orbits between equilibria is another main challenge, which we at least partially address in this paper. 1.4  Main results of the paper We now summarise the main results and techniques of this paper. We show that • for $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small, $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ , $\mu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ , and $c_{0}>0$ , the system ( 1.11 ) has a finite-dimensional, locally invariant centre manifold, which contains all small bounded solutions to ( 1.11 ) of size $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3/4+\delta})$ with $0<\delta<\tfrac{1}{4}$ and the reduced equations on the centre manifold are, to leading order, given by ( 1.6 ), see Theorem 3.10 ; • for $\mu_{0}>0$ , $K_{0}<0$ , $K_{2}<-\tfrac{K_{0}}{2}$ , and $c_{0}>0$ , the reduced equations on the centre manifold have persistent heteroclinic orbits connecting a non-trivial equilibrium to the trivial equilibrium, which correspond to an invasion of the spatially homogeneous state by a planar pattern in ( 1.1 ), see Figure 2 and Theorem 4.14 . a Pattern interfaces for angle $\theta=0$ . b Pattern interfaces for angle $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Figure 2 : The pattern interfaces in panels ( 2a ) and ( 2b ) show two examples of rigorously constructed solutions to ( 1.1 ) in Theorem 4.14 . They describe the invasion of the spatially homogeneous state by down-hexagons and roll waves, respectively. Remark 1.3. We focus on the analysis for patterns, which are periodic with respect to a hexagonal lattice. However, the same analysis can also be done for square patterns, see Section 5 . In fact, this case is less challenging since the corresponding Fourier lattice is non-resonant. Remark 1.4. We note that due to rotation and reflection symmetry, it is sufficient to consider directions with angle $\theta\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{6}]$ . Spectral Analysis The key insight for the spectral analysis is that the eigenvalues of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}$ can be characterised through the linearisation of the Swift–Hohenberg equation ( 1.1 ), specifically, through roots of $L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda$ and their multiplicity, cf. Lemmas 2.1 and 2.2 . This is not a new observation and has been pointed out, for example, in [ AM95 ] . For two-dimensional fronts, this allows for a direct geometric interpretation: if the line $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:=\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\lambda\,:\,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}$ intersects the unit circle, the corresponding $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ has only purely imaginary eigenvalues with imaginary parts given by the distance between the intersection points and $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ , see Figure 5 . In particular, the critical Fourier modes yield eigenvalues at zero. Moreover, if $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ has no intersection points, the eigenvalues of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ have real part with absolute value given by the minimal distance of $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ to the circle. In particular, this shows that there is a spectral gap around the imaginary axis for $\varepsilon=0$ if and only if the lattice is periodic in direction $\mathbf{d}$ , which is equivalent to the condition $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ . In addition to the value of the eigenvalues, the geometric picture also characterises their algebraic multiplicity, which is given by the multiplicity of the root of $\lambda\mapsto L^{0}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ . So, the eigenvalues belong to two Jordan blocks of size two if the line $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ has two or zero intersections with the circle or a Jordan block of size four if $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ is tangent to the circle, using the generic structure of the Turing instability. For $\varepsilon>0$ , the eigenvalues on the imaginary axis depart at different speeds in $\varepsilon$ . Indeed, any eigenvalues emerging from zero have real part $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ and any eigenvalues emerging from a non-zero point on the imaginary axis have real part $\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})$ . The only exception is the case $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , where there are Jordan blocks of size four at zero corresponding to $\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\pm\mathbf{k}_{2}$ and which split into one eigenvalue with real part $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ and three eigenvalues with real part $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1/3})$ . Again, this is a generic effect, since the system is close to a (rotation-symmetric) Turing instability, cf. Appendix A . The spectral picture for $\varepsilon>0$ is thus as follows: there are finitely many more-central eigenvalues with real part $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ , and the remaining eigenvalues split into less-central eigenvalues with real part $\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})$ and hyperbolic eigenvalues. Centre manifold reduction The next step is to prove that there is a locally invariant centre manifold associated with the finitely many more-central eigenvalues. As discussed above, the main obstruction is the non-uniform spectral gap. Therefore, one has to carefully track the dependence on $\varepsilon$ throughout the proof of the centre manifold theorem. This has been done before, mainly for systems with one unbounded spatial direction, see e.g. [ HS99 ] , and the main steps are as follows: first, we split the unknown variable into a more-central, less-central, and hyperbolic part. Second, we introduce rescaled variables, where the more-central, less-central, and hyperbolic parts are rescaled with suitable fractional powers of $\varepsilon$ , and which allow us to determine the size of the centre manifold. The third step is then to show that the resulting rescaled system has a centre manifold of size $\mathcal{O}(1)$ . For this, we first apply a normal-form transformation to remove problematic quadratic nonlinear terms with a singular scaling in $\varepsilon$ . Here, new effects emerge in the two-dimensional case since the typical non-resonance condition in the normal-form analysis, cf. [ GH83 , Sec. 3.3] , is not satisfied uniformly in $\varepsilon$ . Nevertheless, it is possible to obtain a near-identity normal-form transformation, cf. ( 3.9 ) and Lemma 3.5 by exploiting the $\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ -behaviour of the less-central eigenvalues. The second step is to obtain sufficient semigroup estimates, cf. Lemma 3.9 , and to construct a centre manifold using the usual fixed-point construction. Here, the main obstruction in two dimensions is that the semigroups are analytic in $\mathbf{d}^{\perp}$ -direction, but do not gain enough regularity in $\mathbf{d}$ -direction to compensate for more than one derivative in $\mathbf{d}$ -direction. Therefore, the fact that the nonlinearities of the spatial-dynamics system ( 1.11 ) only contain $\mathbf{d}^{\perp}$ -derivatives becomes crucial. To exploit this structure, we perform the fixed-point argument in periodic Sobolev spaces with mixed dominating smoothness, see ( 3.1 ). Carefully tracking the dependence on $\varepsilon$ then yields a centre manifold of size $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3/4+\delta})$ for $\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{4})$ , cf. Theorem 3.10 . This specifically is large enough to contain solutions to ( 1.1 ) of size $\varepsilon$ , which covers the pattern-dynamics near onset, cf. ( 1.4 ). Notably, the resulting centre manifold is smaller than in the one-dimensional case, cf. [ HS99 ] , where the size is of order $\varepsilon^{2/3+\delta}$ . This difference originates directly from the degenerate normal-form transformation, which yields an additional condition $2\beta-\tfrac{1}{2}>1$ from the Lipschitz constant of the less-central nonlinearity, cf. Lemma 3.7 , which is not present in the one-dimensional case. On the centre manifold, we then derive the reduced equation $\begin{split}4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{1}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}+\mu_{0}A_{1}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{2}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}+\mu_{0}A_{2}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{3}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}+\mu_{0}A_{3}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\end{split}$ (1.12) which in particular agrees to leading order with the travelling-wave amplitude equations ( 1.6 ). Although the derivation is mostly standard, we introduce a systematic change of variables exploiting the Jordan-block structure at $\varepsilon=0$ , see ( 3.25 ). This agrees with the ad-hoc change of variables used, for example, in [ EW91 ] , but makes the connection to the algebraic structure of the eigenvalues more transparent. Note that there are two special cases, which require separate treatment. The first case is $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . As discussed above, in this case, $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})$ has an eigenvalue at zero corresponding to a Jordan block of size four, which splits into one more-central and three less-central eigenvalues. Therefore, in contrast to the generic case $\theta\neq\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , we cannot define uniformly bounded projections separating the more-central and less-central eigenvalues. However, it is still possible to construct a centre manifold of the same size, cf. Section 3.5 , by defining non-normalised ’projection-like’ maps, see ( 3.34 ), which still map into the relevant eigenspace. With this, the rest of the proof works the same as in the generic-angle case and the reduced equations on the centre manifold are again given by ( 1.12 ) with $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2}=0$ . The second case is $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , where $c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j}):=4|\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}|\sqrt{\mu_{0}}$ (1.13) is the selected speed predicted by marginal stability analysis, cf. Section 1.2 . Notably, it can be obtained from ( 1.8 ) where the transverse wave number $k_{\perp}$ is given by $\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}$ . In the spectral analysis, this speed yields a different $\varepsilon$ -expansion of the more-central eigenvalues. Specifically, for $c_{0}\neq c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ for all $j=1,2,3$ , the more-central eigenvalues coming from $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ split at leading order $\varepsilon$ for all $j=1,2,3$ . In contrast, for $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ for fixed $j=1,2,3$ the leading-order behaviour of the more-central eigenvalues from $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is the same and they only split at order $\varepsilon^{3/2}$ . While this does not affect the construction of the centre manifold, since the more-central eigenvalues still are of order $\varepsilon$ , it changes the derivation of the reduced equations, see Section 3.6 . However, the resulting leading-order system on the centre manifold is still ( 1.6 ). Indeed, this is expected since the derivation of the amplitude system ( 1.5 ) is done in the stationary frame. Nevertheless, as pointed out in Section 4.1.3 , the dynamics of the reduced equation still change when $c_{0}$ passes through $c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ for some $j=1,2,3$ . Dynamics of reduced equations and pattern interfaces Finally, we analyse the dynamics of the reduced system ( 1.12 ) on the centre manifold. For this, we restrict the corresponding twelve-dimensional (note the complex conjugate equations) first-order system to the invariant, six-dimensional subspace of real-valued solutions. For the special direction $\theta=0$ , there is an additional four-dimensional invariant subspace, see [ Doe+03 ] . However, to deal with general directions, we analyse the full six-dimensional system. We first collect non-trivial equilibria, which correspond to planar patterns in ( 1.1 ) and which can be obtained using standard results from equivariant bifurcation theory. To analyse their stability, we establish a rigorous relation between the temporal stability of an equilibrium as a stationary state in the Ginzburg–Landau system ( 1.5 ) without diffusion, and the stability in the spatial dynamics system on the centre manifold, see Lemma 4.4 . Specifically, temporally fully unstable equilibria are stable in the reduced equation on the centre manifold, and temporally stable equilibria are saddles with an even splitting between stable and unstable directions. In particular, this implies that for $\mu_{0}>0$ , the trivial equilibrium is stable. While this characterisation was previously noted, see e.g. [ Doe+03 ] , to the best of our knowledge, a rigorous proof is not available in the literature. Moreover, we discuss that the result also applies to reaction-diffusion systems when the linearisation about an equilibrium is symmetric; otherwise, it might fail, see Remark 4.6 . To obtain heteroclinic orbits, we use that the leading-order first-order system has a strictly decreasing Lyapunov function, see ( 4.14 ). Restricting to the case $\mu_{0}>0$ , $c_{0}>0$ , $K_{0}<0$ and $K_{2}<-\tfrac{K_{0}}{2}$ , we use this to prove the existence of heteroclinic orbits between the non-trivial equilibrium with lowest energy and the (stable) trivial equilibrium, see Theorem 4.10 for the case $\theta\neq\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . The proof relies on constructing an appropriate bounded trapping region around the trivial equilibrium using the Lyapunov function. Their persistence then follows using the transverse intersection of stable and unstable manifolds, see Theorem 4.12 . Finally, using fast-slow analysis, we show that these orbits converge to a heteroclinic orbit connecting the same patterns along a sequence of angles $\theta$ satisfying $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ and converging to $\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Similarly, the fast-moving pattern interfaces obtained in [ HJ25 ] can be recovered in the limit $c_{0}\to\infty$ as expected. In addition to the rigorous analysis, we find heteroclinic orbits using a numerical shooting algorithm, which yields orbits connecting the other non-trivial equilibrium to the trivial state, see Figures 1 and 12 for the corresponding planar interfaces. In particular, we find orbits from up-hexagons to the trivial state, which pass close to the roll waves and thus correspond to a two-stage invasion described in Section 1.2 where the primary and secondary invasion fronts have the same speed, see Figure 12c . 1.5  Related results We now discuss related results. As mentioned above, the existence of pattern interfaces using spatial dynamics and centre manifold theory is well-known for systems in an infinite cylinder. Specifically, they have been constructed for the one-dimensional Swift–Hohenberg equation [ EW91 ] , the Taylor–Couette problem [ HS99 ] , and a non-local reaction-diffusion system [ FH15 ] . In the case of a Turing(–Hopf) instability with additional conservation law, which for example appears in the Bénard–Marangoni problem, one-dimensional pattern interfaces have been found in phenomenological models [ Hil20 , Hil22 ] . Recently, the existence, stability, and selection of related (steady) front solutions have also been studied in the FitzHugh–Nagumo system [ Ave+25 , ACR26 ] . In systems with two unbounded spatial directions, results are sparser. Besides the existence result for planar pattern interfaces in [ Doe+03 ] , much attention has been focused on stationary interfaces between differently oriented roll waves, which are also known as grain boundary or domain walls. Their existence has been obtained for a two-dimensional Swift–Hohenberg equation [ HS07 , HS12 ] and, more recently, for the Rayleigh–Bénard problem [ HI21 , HI22 , BHI23 , Ioo25 , Ioo26 ] . While these results are restricted to semilinear systems, fast-moving pattern interfaces with $c=\mathcal{O}(1)$ have recently been obtained in a quasilinear asymptotic thin-film model for the Bénard–Marangoni problem [ HJ25 ] . We expect that the generic approach of this paper can also be transferred to construct slow-moving pattern interfaces in these models. However, there are substantial additional difficulties which we discuss in more detail in Section 6 . All of the previously mentioned results used a spatial-dynamics approach to obtain the existence of pattern interfaces. This approach does not directly translate to systems that exhibit multiple, simultaneous pattern-forming instabilities which occur at resonant wave numbers. Here, difficulties arise from additional interactions of the critical modes. For these systems, the existence of solutions in one spatial dimension that are close to pattern interfaces at least on a long time interval has been established using modulation theory [ Gau+21 , HK25 ] . The study of steady localised solutions to pattern-forming systems has received much interest in recent years, and we refer to [ BHL25 ] for a recent overview. Many of these results rely on numerical continuation methods, for example, obtained using pde2path [ Uec21 ] , see [ UW14 ] for an application. While these references mainly consider stationary solutions and the existence of radial front solutions remains a largely open question, we highlight the recent derivation of radial amplitude equations [ HL24 ] . Numerical continuation methods in combination with far-field-core decompositions have also been used recently to study the behaviour of invading one-dimensional pattern interfaces beyond onset [ LGR25 ] . 1.6  Outline of the paper The paper is structured as follows: In Section 2 , we perform a spectral analysis of the linearity in the spatial-dynamics system ( 1.11 ) by first analysing the spectrum for $\varepsilon=0$ , and then characterising the behaviour of the eigenvalues for $\varepsilon>0$ . In Section 3 , we prove a centre manifold theorem for the spatial-dynamics system ( 1.11 ) and derive the reduced equations. For this, we first introduce an appropriate functional-analytic setup based on mixed-regularity Sobolev spaces and then perform a normal-form analysis in a rescaled system. Then, we prove the necessary bounds on the semigroups to obtain a sufficiently large centre manifold with a standard fixed-point argument. In the analysis, we first consider the generic case $\theta\neq\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ and $c_{0}\neq c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ . Then, we provide a proof of the centre manifold theorem in the special case $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , and a derivation of the reduced equation in the case $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ separately. Section 4 then contains the analysis of the dynamics of the reduced equations on the centre manifold. Here, we first discuss the equilibria which correspond to planar patterns in ( 1.1 ), and characterise their stability. Afterwards, we prove the existence of persistent heteroclinic orbits and use this to establish the existence of slow-moving pattern interfaces in ( 1.1 ), see Theorem 4.14 . In Section 5 , we discuss how our results transfer from the hexagonal lattice case to the square lattice case. We conclude the main body of the paper by discussing related questions in Section 6 . In Appendix A , we then provide a detailed discussion of how the spectral results apply more generally to generic, rotation-invariant pattern-forming systems close to a Turing instability. Finally, Appendix B contains numerical results on the selected front speed. 2  Spectral analysis We now analyse the spectrum of the linear part of ( 1.11 ). Although the calculations are done for the linear operator given by the Swift–Hohenberg-type equation ( 1.1 ), the results are fully generic for rotationally symmetric pattern-forming systems close to a Turing instability, albeit with more involved notation. We outline this in detail in Appendix A . Using the specific form of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ and that $i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}\hat{W}$ only introduces a purely imaginary shift, we can relate the spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ to the spectrum of $L^{\varepsilon}$ , cf. [ EW91 ] . Lemma 2.1. Let $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ . Then, $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ is an eigenvalue of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ if and only if $L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda=0$ . The corresponding eigenvector is then given by $(1,\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma},(\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{2},(\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{3})^{T}$ . Proof. We first note that $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ is an eigenvalue of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ if and only if $\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ is an eigenvalue of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ . By standard construction, see e.g. [ EW91 ] , the corresponding eigenvector for the latter eigenvalue problem is $(1,\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma},(\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{2},(\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{3})^{T}$ . Then, since the problems only differ by a multiple of the identity, the eigenvectors are identical. Since ( 1.11 ) and ( 1.10 ) are equivalent by construction, we also find that the linearised equations $\partial_{\xi}\hat{W}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})\hat{W}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}\hat{W}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ (2.1) and $(\partial_{\xi}+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{4}\hat{U}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\varepsilon c_{0}\partial_{\xi}\hat{U}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})+L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})\hat{U}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})+(\partial_{\xi}+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})\hat{U}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ (2.2) are equivalent. Therefore, $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ is an eigenvalue of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}I$ if and only if $\hat{W}=e^{\lambda\xi}(1,\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma},(\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{2},(\lambda+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{3})^{T}$ is a solution to ( 2.1 ) if and only if $\hat{U}=e^{\lambda\xi}$ is a solution to ( 2.2 ) if and only if $0=\varepsilon c_{0}\partial_{\xi}\hat{U}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})+L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})\hat{U}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda+L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\partial_{\xi}+i\boldsymbol{\gamma}).$ This completes the proof.
∎ 2.1  Spectral analysis for $\varepsilon=0$ We start the spectral analysis with the case $\varepsilon=0$ and then analyse the behaviour for $\varepsilon>0$ . Since we aim to apply centre manifold theory, we first characterise the purely imaginary eigenvalues of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}$ . Applying Lemma 2.1 , we find that $\lambda=i\lambda_{i}$ with $\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}$ is in the spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ for some $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ if and only if $L^{0}(i(\mathbf{d}\lambda_{i}+\boldsymbol{\gamma}))=\hat{L}^{0}(\mathbf{d}\lambda_{i}+\boldsymbol{\gamma})=0$ . Here $\hat{L}^{0}(\mathbf{k})=-(1-|\mathbf{k}|^{2})^{2}$ , $\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ , is the Fourier symbol of $L^{0}$ . In particular, $i\lambda_{i}$ is in the spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\gamma)$ if and only if $|\mathbf{d}\lambda_{i}+\boldsymbol{\gamma}|=1$ . Therefore, the purely imaginary eigenvalues of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ can be determined geometrically, see Figures 3 and 4 . This geometric interpretation in particular yields that there are infinitely many purely imaginary eigenvalues of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}$ , which is given as the direct sum of the $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ over the Fourier modes $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ . Due to the discreteness of the Fourier lattice $\Gamma$ , we also obtain that these imaginary eigenvalues are discrete, that is, there is a uniform neighbourhood around every imaginary eigenvalue not containing any other eigenvalue on the imaginary axis. Finally, we discuss the multiplicity of the imaginary eigenvalues. For this, we use the following result, cf. [ HJ25 , Lem. 5.4] . Lemma 2.2. Let $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ and let $i\lambda_{i}\in i\mathbb{R}$ be an eigenvalue of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ . Then, the geometric multiplicity of $i\lambda_{i}$ is one. Additionally, its algebraic multiplicity is $m_{0}$ if $\hat{L}^{0}(\mathbf{d}(\lambda_{i}+\tilde{\lambda})+\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\kappa\tilde{\lambda}^{m_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(|\tilde{\lambda}|^{m_{0}+1})$ with $\kappa\neq 0$ . For eigenvalues on the imaginary axis, Lemma 2.1 allows for a convenient geometric interpretation of the eigenvalue problem for $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}$ . That is, $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ has an imaginary eigenvalue $i\lambda_{i}$ for some $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ if the line $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:=\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\lambda\,:\,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}$ intersects the unit circle $S^{1}$ . Since $|\mathbf{d}|=1$ , $\lambda_{i}$ is the distance between $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ and the intersection point. Note that, for fixed $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ , three different scenarios can occur: the line $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ can have zero, one or two intersection points with the unit circle. In the first case, $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ has no purely imaginary eigenvalue. In the second case, $\ell$ intersects the unit circle tangentially and thus, $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ has one purely imaginary eigenvalue. Finally, in the third case, $\ell$ has two intersections with the unit circle and $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ has two distinct purely imaginary eigenvalues. Together with Lemma 2.2 , this geometric point of view also allows us to determine the algebraic multiplicity of the purely imaginary eigenvalues. For this, we calculate using $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\lambda\in S^{1}$ $\hat{L}^{0}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\mathbf{d}\tilde{\lambda})=-(1-|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\mathbf{d}\tilde{\lambda}|^{2})^{2}=-(\tilde{\lambda}^{2}+2\tilde{\lambda}\mathbf{d}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})^{2}$ Therefore, if $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ has two intersection points with the unit circle, then $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\cdot\mathbf{d}\neq 0$ and $\hat{L}^{0}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\mathbf{d}\tilde{\lambda})$ is quadratic to lowest order in $\tilde{\lambda}$ . Hence, in this case, $i\lambda_{i}$ is an eigenvalue with algebraic multiplicity two. In particular, the purely imaginary spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ consists of two Jordan blocks of size two. If the intersection of $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ with the unit circle is tangential, then $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ is orthogonal to $\mathbf{d}$ . Thus, $i\lambda_{i}$ is an eigenvalue with algebraic multiplicity four of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ and belongs to a single Jordan block of size four. By applying these observations to the Fourier lattice $\Gamma$ , we obtain the following result. Proposition 2.3. The imaginary spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ is discrete, and all imaginary eigenvalues belong to a Jordan block of size at least two. The origin $\lambda=0$ is an eigenvalue of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ for $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ if and only if $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{0}$ . In particular, $\lambda=0$ is an eigenvalue of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ with finite multiplicity. Furthermore, $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ has a Jordan block of size four at $\lambda=0$ if and only if $\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\pm\mathbf{k}_{2}$ and $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Otherwise, $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{j})$ has two Jordan blocks of size two at $\lambda=0$ and $0\neq\lambda\in i\mathbb{R}$ . Up to rotational symmetry, there is no other angle $\theta$ for which a Jordan block of size four occurs for $\lambda=0$ . Since the spectrum on the imaginary axis is now fully characterised, it remains to understand the spectrum outside the imaginary axis. Specifically, we are interested in the presence of a spectral gap. It turns out that this is highly dependent on the angle $\theta$ as the following result states. Proposition 2.4. The operator $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ has a spectral gap around the imaginary axis if and only if $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ . Furthermore, if $\cot(\theta)\notin\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ , any sequence of eigenvalues $\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus i\mathbb{R}$ with $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{n})\rightarrow 0$ satisfies $|\operatorname{Im}(\lambda_{n})|\rightarrow\infty$ . Proof. We recall from Lemma 2.1 that for any eigenvalue $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ , there exists a $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ such that $L^{0}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})=-(1+\lambda^{2}-|\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}+2i\lambda\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{2}=0.$ Solutions to this can be explicitly given by $\lambda_{\pm}=-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}\pm\sqrt{-|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}-1}.$ (2.3) Therefore, $\lambda_{\pm}$ has a non-trivial real part if and only if $-|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}-1>0$ . In this case, $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{\pm})=\pm\sqrt{-|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}-1}=\pm\sqrt{|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}-1},$ where $\mathbf{d}\perp\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\in S^{1}$ and we used $|\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}=|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}+|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|^{2}$ . This allows for the following geometric interpretation: $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|$ gives the distance of $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ to the line $\{\mathbf{d}s\,:\,s\in\mathbb{R}\}$ . Therefore, if $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>1$ the shifted line $\{\mathbf{d}s+\boldsymbol{\gamma}\,:\,s\in\mathbb{R}\}$ has no intersections with the unit circle, see Figure 3 . Hence, it suffices to understand the set $\{|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|\,:\,\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma\}$ . Since $\mathbf{d}^{\perp}=(-\sin(\theta),\cos(\theta))$ , for $\boldsymbol{\gamma}=n_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1}+n_{2}\mathbf{k}_{2}\in\Gamma$ we obtain $\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}=-n_{1}\sin(\theta)+n_{2}(-\sin(\theta)\cos(\tfrac{2\pi}{3})+\cos(\theta)\sin(\tfrac{2\pi}{3}))=-n_{1}\sin(\theta)-n_{2}\sin(\theta-\tfrac{2\pi}{3})$ using the addition theorems. We now make the following claim: the set $\{n_{1}\sin(\theta)+n_{2}\sin(\theta-\tfrac{2\pi}{3})\,:\,k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\}$ is discrete in $\mathbb{R}$ if and only if $\theta\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ . Otherwise, this set is dense in $\mathbb{R}$ . Observe that this implies the first part of the proposition. To prove the claim, we note that we can equivalently prove the claim for the set $\{n_{1}+n_{2}\tfrac{\sin(\theta-2\pi/3)}{\sin(\theta)}\,:\,k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\}=\mathbb{Z}+a\mathbb{Z}$ with $a=\tfrac{\sin(\theta-2\pi/3)}{\sin(\theta)}$ , since in the case $\theta=0$ there is nothing to prove. Recall that $\mathbb{Z}+a\mathbb{Z}$ is discrete in $\mathbb{R}$ if and only if $a\in\mathbb{Q}$ , and otherwise it is dense. Finally, we observe that $\tfrac{\sin(\theta-2\pi/3)}{\sin(\theta)}=-\frac{1}{2}-\sqrt{3}\cot(\theta)\in\mathbb{Q}$ if and only if $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ .
∎ a b Figure 3 : Geometric characterisation of the spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}$ : lattice points in the blue shaded strip generate eigenvalues on the imaginary axis ( 3a ) , while lattice points outside generate hyperbolic eigenvalues ( 3b ) . a b Figure 4 : Spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}$ for $\theta$ satisfying $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ . Here, all hyperbolic lattice points have a positive distance to the blue shaded strip ( 4a ) , which yields a spectral gap around the imaginary axis ( 4b ) . 2.2  Spectral analysis for $\varepsilon>0$ We now want to understand the spectrum of the operator $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ for $\varepsilon>0$ . In particular, we want to show that there is a strip with a size of order $\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ around the imaginary axis that contains only finitely many eigenvalues, which emerge precisely from the eigenvalue curves for $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{0}$ and have a real part of order $\varepsilon$ . It turns out that the analysis presented here strongly depends on the presence of a spectral gap around the imaginary axis at $\varepsilon=0$ . Therefore, we restrict to angles $\theta$ satisfying $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ , see Proposition 2.4 . The analysis is split into three cases: first, we handle the eigenvalues of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{j})\mp i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}I$ for $j\in 1,2,3$ , second, we consider the eigenvalues which are on the imaginary axis at $\varepsilon=0$ , and finally, we deal with the eigenvalues with a non-trivial real part at $\varepsilon=0$ . While the first case can be handled with an implicit function theorem argument similar to [ Hil20 ] , the latter two cases require a more subtle argument to guarantee that there is a uniform gap of order $\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ . For this, we recall that eigenvalues of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ with $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ are given by the roots of the fourth-order polynomial $p(\lambda;\boldsymbol{\gamma},\varepsilon):=L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda=L^{0}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}+\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda,$ (2.4) with $L^{0}(\mathbf{v})=-(1+\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{v})^{2}$ . We note that $p(\lambda;\boldsymbol{\gamma},0)$ has two double roots or one fourth root. The latter always lies on the imaginary axis. Note that for $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\notin\Gamma_{0}$ all roots are of the form $\lambda_{\pm}=-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\gamma\pm\Delta(\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ , see ( 2.3 ), where $\Delta$ is real if $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>1$ and purely imaginary otherwise. Under the assumption that $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ , it follows that there is $\delta>0$ such that $|\Delta(\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})|>\delta$ for all $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{0}$ with $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\cdot\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\neq 1$ . The analysis of the spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ relies on a careful expansion using the implicit function theorem, which is only valid locally. This is combined with two results about the location of the roots of the polynomial $p$ that guarantee that eventually sufficient uniform spectral gaps hold true. The following lemma guarantees that the eigenvalue curves starting at purely imaginary eigenvalues will eventually be sufficiently far away from the imaginary axis. This includes the fourth-order Jordan blocks. Lemma 2.5. For every $c_{1}>0$ sufficiently small there are $\varepsilon_{0}>0$ and $r_{1}>0$ such that for every $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ and every $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ with $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|\leqslant 1$ with $\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>c_{1}$ it holds that $p(\lambda;\boldsymbol{\gamma},\varepsilon)=0$ implies that $|\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)|>r_{1}$ . Proof. Using that $L^{0}$ has roots at $\lambda_{\pm}=-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}\pm\Delta$ for some $\Delta=i\Delta_{i}\in i\mathbb{R}$ with $|\Delta_{i}|\leqslant 1$ , we can write $p(\lambda;\boldsymbol{\gamma},\varepsilon)=-(\lambda+i(\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})+i\Delta_{i})^{2}(\lambda+i(\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})-i\Delta_{i})^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}+\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda.$ We now replace $\lambda=\tilde{\lambda}-i(\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})$ to obtain $g(\tilde{\lambda}):=-(\tilde{\lambda}+i\Delta_{i})^{2}(\tilde{\lambda}-i\Delta_{i})^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}+\varepsilon c_{0}\tilde{\lambda}-i\varepsilon c_{0}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}).$ Now, fix any $c_{1}>0$ . Then, we show the claim in two steps. First, we show that for any $C_{i}>0$ sufficiently large, there exist $\varepsilon_{0}>0$ and $\tilde{r}_{1}$ such that for any $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ it holds that $g(\tilde{\lambda})\neq 0$ if $\tilde{\lambda}\in\{|\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\lambda})|<\tilde{r}_{1}\}\cap\{|\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\lambda})|>C_{i}\}$ . Second, we show that for any $C_{i}>0$ sufficiently large, there exist $\varepsilon_{0}>0$ and $\tilde{r}_{2}>0$ such that for any $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ it holds that $g(\tilde{\lambda})\neq 0$ if $\tilde{\lambda}\in\{|\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\lambda})|<\tilde{r}_{2}\}\cap\{|\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\lambda})|<C_{i}\}$ provided $\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>c_{1}$ . This proves the claim by choosing $r_{1}=\min(\tilde{r}_{1},\tilde{r}_{2})$ . For the first step, we fix $C_{i}>0$ sufficiently large. Then, we note that $\operatorname{Re}(g(\tilde{\lambda}))$ is independent of $\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ and $|\operatorname{Re}(-(\tilde{\lambda}+i\Delta_{i})^{2}(\tilde{\lambda}-i\Delta_{i})^{2})|\sim\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\lambda})^{4}$ for $|\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\lambda})|\rightarrow\infty$ . In particular, since $|\Delta_{i}|\leqslant 1$ for all $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ with $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|\leqslant 1$ , the coefficients of $\operatorname{Re}(g)$ are also uniformly bounded for all such $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ . Therefore, by choosing $\tilde{r}_{1}$ and $\varepsilon_{0}$ sufficiently small, we can guarantee that $\operatorname{Re}(g(\tilde{\lambda}))\neq 0$ and thus $g(\tilde{\lambda})\neq 0$ if $|\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\lambda})|>C_{i}$ and $|\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\lambda})|<\tilde{r}_{1}$ . For the second step, we notice that for $\tilde{\lambda}=i\tilde{\lambda}_{i}$ it holds $g(i\tilde{\lambda}_{i})=-(\tilde{\lambda}_{i}-\Delta_{i})^{2}(\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+\Delta_{i})^{2}+\varepsilon c_{0}i\tilde{\lambda}_{i}-ic_{0}\varepsilon\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}.$ Hence, we obtain $|g(i\tilde{\lambda}_{i})|>|\operatorname{Im}(g(i\tilde{\lambda}_{i})|>c_{0}c_{1}-\varepsilon_{0}C_{i}>\tfrac{c_{0}c_{1}}{2}$ for all $|\tilde{\lambda}_{i}|<C_{i}$ by choosing $\varepsilon_{0}$ sufficiently small and provided $\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>c_{1}$ . Now, a compactness argument guarantees that there is $\tilde{r}_{2}>0$ such that $g(\tilde{\lambda})\neq 0$ for all $\tilde{\lambda}$ with $|\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\lambda})|<\tilde{r}_{2}$ and $|\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\lambda})|<C_{i}$ . This concludes the proof.
∎ In order to guarantee that the real spectral gap persists, we need another lemma in a similar spirit: Lemma 2.6. Assume $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ . There exist $\varepsilon_{0}>0$ and $r_{2}>0$ such that for all $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ with $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>1$ it holds that $p(\lambda;\boldsymbol{\gamma},\varepsilon)=0$ implies that $|\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)|>r_{2}$ for all $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ . Proof. Since $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>1$ , we find that $p(\lambda;\boldsymbol{\gamma},0)$ has two double roots at $\lambda_{0}=-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}\pm\Delta$ for $\Delta\in\mathbb{R}$ . Furthermore, we note that due to $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ , there exists a $\delta>0$ such that $|\Delta|>\delta$ . Similar to the proof of Lemma 2.5 , we write $\lambda=\tilde{\lambda}-i(\mathbf{d}\cdot\gamma)$ and define $f(\tilde{\lambda}):=-(\tilde{\lambda}+\Delta)^{2}(\tilde{\lambda}-\Delta)^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}+\varepsilon c_{0}\tilde{\lambda}-i\varepsilon c_{0}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}).$ We now show that there exist $\varepsilon_{0},r_{2}$ such that $\operatorname{Re}(f(\tilde{\lambda}))\neq 0$ for $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ and $|\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\lambda})|<r_{2}$ . For this, we note that $\operatorname{Re}(f(\tilde{\lambda}))=\operatorname{Re}(-(\tilde{\lambda}+\Delta)^{2}(\tilde{\lambda}-\Delta)^{2})+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}+\varepsilon c_{0}\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\lambda}).$ (2.5) By choosing $r_{2}$ and $\varepsilon_{0}$ sufficiently small, we can thus treat $\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}$ and $\varepsilon c_{0}\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\lambda})$ as small perturbations. Therefore, it is sufficient to guarantee that $\operatorname{Re}((\tilde{\lambda}+\Delta)^{2}(\tilde{\lambda}-\Delta)^{2})\neq 0$ for all $\tilde{\lambda}$ with $|\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\lambda})|<r_{2}$ . As in the proof of Lemma 2.5 , this is true for $\operatorname{Im}(\tilde{\lambda})$ sufficiently large. Hence, it remains to prove that the real part is non-zero in a compact strip around the imaginary axis, which follows from $|\operatorname{Re}(f(i\tilde{\lambda}_{i}))|=(\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{2}+\Delta^{2})^{2}>\delta^{4}$ . Using a compactness argument concludes the proof.
∎ We are now in a position to prove the main result on the behaviour of the spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ for $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small. Proposition 2.7. Let $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ . There is $\varepsilon_{0}>0$ and a constant $C_{0}>0$ such that for $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ the spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ looks like this: (a) if $\theta\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{6})$ , there are twelve eigenvalues $\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\varepsilon\nu_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ , $j=\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3$ of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ with $\nu_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\begin{cases}\frac{-c_{0}\pm\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}&\text{if }c_{0}\neq c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\\[6.0pt]
-\frac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\pm\sqrt{\varepsilon}\frac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}&\text{if }c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\end{cases}$ (2.6) where $c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is given by ( 1.13 ); (b) if $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , there are ten eigenvalues $\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\varepsilon\nu_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ for $j=\pm 1,\pm 3$ and $\lambda_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})=\varepsilon\nu_{\perp}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ with $\nu_{\perp}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{c_{0}};$ (2.7) (c) all other eigenvalues $\lambda_{h}(\varepsilon)$ of the operator $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ satisfy $|\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{h}(\varepsilon))|>C_{0}\sqrt{\varepsilon}.$ Remark 2.8. Note that we need to distinguish the case that $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is the conjectured selected front speed, cf. Section 1.2 , in the expansion ( 2.6 ). In this case, the Jordan block splits only at higher order, which creates additional difficulties in the centre manifold analysis and the derivation of the corresponding reduced equations. We refer to Section 3.6 for additional details. Proof. We first recall from Lemma 2.1 that $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ is an eigenvalue of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}$ if there exists a $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma$ such that $L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\boldsymbol{\gamma})+\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda=0$ . To prove the first statement, we assume that $\theta\neq\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ and thus $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}\neq 0$ for all $j=\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3$ . Then, we look for solutions to $L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\mathbf{k}_{j})+\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda=0$ for $\lambda$ close to zero. Using that $|\mathbf{k}_{j}|=1$ we find that $L^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\mathbf{k}_{j})=-(\lambda^{2}+2i\lambda\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}.$ We note that for $\varepsilon=0$ , eigenvalues are roots of the fourth-order polynomial $L^{0}(\mathbf{d}\lambda+i\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , which has a double root at $\lambda=0$ and a double root at $-2i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}$ . Hence, for $\varepsilon>0$ there are exactly two solutions close to zero. To find these, we may consider the lowest-order powers of $\lambda$ and make the ansatz $\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\varepsilon\nu$ , which yields $4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\nu^{2}+c_{0}\nu+\mu_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon|\nu|^{3})=0.$ (2.8) By solving the leading-order equation, we thus find that if $c_{0}\neq c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , the solutions are given by $\nu_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\frac{-c_{0}\pm\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}.$ Applying the implicit function theorem as in [ Hil20 ] , these solutions persist, and we find two eigenvalues of the form $\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\varepsilon\nu_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ . If $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , we make the extended ansatz $\lambda^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\varepsilon\nu_{1}+\varepsilon^{3/2}\nu_{2}$ . The leading-order equation is then solved by $\nu_{1}=\nu_{1}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=-\tfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})}$ . Additionally, noting that the $\varepsilon^{5/2}$ -terms vanish due to $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ and equating terms of order $\varepsilon^{3}$ to zero, we find that $\nu_{2,\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\pm\dfrac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}.$ Again, the implicit function theorem yields that these solutions persist, and we obtain two eigenvalues of the form $\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\varepsilon\nu_{1}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\varepsilon^{3/2}\nu_{2,\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=:\varepsilon\nu_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ . To prepare the proof of the third statement, we now also show that the eigenvalues of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}I$ close to $-2i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}$ have real part of order $\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ . Again, there are exactly two solutions close to $-2i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}$ and we therefore make the ansatz $\lambda_{h}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=-2i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\tilde{\nu}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ . Inserting this yields $0=-(2i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\tilde{\nu})^{2}\varepsilon\tilde{\nu}^{2}+\varepsilon c_{0}(-2i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\tilde{\nu})+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}=\varepsilon(4(d\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\tilde{\nu}^{2}-2i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}c_{0})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\tfrac{3}{2}}).$ Therefore, we find to leading order that $\tilde{\nu}_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\pm\sqrt{\tfrac{ic_{0}}{2\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}}}$ . Again, an application of the implicit function theorem shows that these persist under higher-order perturbations, which shows the desired behaviour of the eigenvalue curves since $|\operatorname{Re}(\tilde{\nu}_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))|>0$ . We now assume that $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ and thus $\mathbf{d}\cdot(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})=0$ . The only difference from the case $\theta\neq\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ is the eigenvalues of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})\mp i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2}I$ , which satisfy $-\lambda^{4}+\varepsilon c_{0}\lambda+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}=0.$ Therefore, as already shown in Lemma 2.2 , $\lambda=0$ is a fourth-order root at $\varepsilon=0$ . We now show that this splits into one eigenvalue $\lambda_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})$ with real part of order $\varepsilon$ and three eigenvalues with real part of order $\varepsilon^{1/3}$ . Indeed, making the ansatz $\lambda=\varepsilon\nu_{\perp}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})$ we find, to leading order, $\nu_{\perp}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{c_{0}},$ while we obtain a third-order polynomial, to leading order, when making the ansatz $\lambda=\varepsilon^{1/3}\tilde{\nu}$ , which therefore yields three solutions $\tilde{\nu}_{1,2,3}$ . Again, following a similar strategy as in [ Hil20 ] , the use of the implicit function theorem shows the persistence of all solution branches and therefore the claim. It thus remains to show the third statement. We first consider the case $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|\leqslant 1$ . We first restrict to the case $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|<1$ which yields that $\Delta_{i}>0$ . We start by showing that for $\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|<c_{1}$ for $c_{1}$ sufficiently small the expansions for the four eigenvalues of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}I$ $\lambda_{h,+}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}+i\Delta_{i}\pm\sqrt{\varepsilon(\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\Delta_{i})}\dfrac{\sqrt{ic_{0}}}{2\Delta_{i}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}+1|)$ (2.9) hold. Similarly for $\lambda_{h,-}$ . For this, we replace $\lambda_{h}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}+i\Delta_{i}+\nu_{h}=i\lambda_{0}+\nu_{h}$ which yields $0=-\nu_{h}^{2}(\nu_{h}+2i\Delta_{i})^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}+\varepsilon c_{0}(i\lambda_{0}+\nu_{h})=-\nu_{h}^{2}(\nu_{h}+2i\Delta_{i})^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{\lambda_{0}^{2}}+\varepsilon c_{0}i\lambda_{0}+\varepsilon\lambda_{0}c_{0}\dfrac{\nu_{h}}{\lambda_{0}}.$ Again, we replace $\nu_{h}=\sqrt{\varepsilon\lambda_{0}}\tilde{\nu}_{h}$ and consider the lowest power in $\varepsilon\lambda_{0}$ . This gives $0=4\Delta_{i}^{2}\tilde{\nu}_{h}^{2}+ic_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\lambda_{0})$ and therefore $\tilde{\nu}_{h}=\pm\tfrac{\sqrt{-ic_{0}}}{2\Delta_{i}}$ . Again, applying the implicit function theorem and using $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\lambda_{0})=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}+1|)$ , we obtain the desired expansion ( 2.9 ). In particular, this implies that there exists a constant $C_{0}>0$ , which is independent of $\gamma$ such that $|\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{h,+}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))|>C_{0}\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ for $\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|<c_{1}$ with $c_{1}$ sufficiently small. Next, we consider the case that $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|=1$ and $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\neq\pm\mathbf{k}_{2}$ , which corresponds to a Jordan block of size four. In this case, we find that $\nu_{h}=\lambda_{h}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ satisfies $0=-\nu_{h}^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}+\varepsilon c_{0}(\lambda_{0}+\nu_{h}).$ As expected from the Jordan block of size four, this suggests the rescaling $\nu_{h}=(\varepsilon\lambda_{0})^{1/4}\tilde{\nu}_{h}$ . Proceeding with this as in the case $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|<1$ we thus find to lowest order in $\varepsilon\lambda_{0}$ that $\tilde{\nu}_{h}^{4}=ic_{0}$ . Again, the implicit function theorem implies persistence of these solutions, and we obtain that $|\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{h,+}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))|>C_{0}\varepsilon^{1/4}>C_{0}\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ for $\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|<c_{1}$ and $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small. This completes the proof in the case $\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|<c_{1}$ . If, on the other hand, $\varepsilon|\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>c_{1}$ , we can use Lemma 2.5 to obtain that $|\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{h,+}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))|>r_{1}$ for some $r_{1}>0$ independent of $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ . Finally, if $|\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}|>1$ , then Lemma 2.6 yields that $|\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{h}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}))|\geqslant r_{2}$ for some $r_{2}>0$ independent of $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ . This concludes the proof of the third statement.
∎ 3  Centre manifold theory and reduced equations We now establish a centre manifold theorem following the analysis in [ HS99 ] and pay particular attention to the size of the centre manifold. We first restrict to the case $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ with $\theta\neq\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , where the more-central eigenvalues belong to Jordan blocks at zero of size 2. We then discuss the extension to the case $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , when there are more-central eigenvalues belonging to a Jordan block of size 4 in Section 3.5 . The derivation of the reduced equations in the case $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is done in Section 3.6 . The analysis is split into different steps: first, we define the necessary projections onto the more-central, less-central, and hyperbolic parts and introduce rescaled variables by using different rescalings for the different parts of the solution. These rescaled variables make the $\varepsilon$ -dependence in the centre manifold analysis explicit and allow us to show that the centre manifold is indeed large enough to contain interesting solutions. Next, we perform a normal-form analysis to remove the terms with the lowest-order scaling in $\varepsilon$ . Finally, we show that we can close a fixed-point argument to obtain a centre manifold, which is of size $\mathcal{O}(1)$ in the rescaled variables.
