&=\bigl(1,i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j},(i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2},(i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{3}\bigr)^{T}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\end{split}$ see [ EW91 ] . Therefore, for $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{0}$ with $d(\boldsymbol{\gamma},0)\leqslant 2$ , we find that the leading-order contribution of $(\tilde{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})_{0}$ is given as the solution to $\hat{L}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})(\tilde{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})_{0}=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,\ell=-3\\
j,\ell\neq 0\end{subarray}}^{3}\delta_{\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}}A_{j}A_{\ell}\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\boldsymbol{\gamma},\vartheta).$ Here, we note that $\hat{L}^{0}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})\neq 0$ for $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{0}$ . For later use, we thus introduce the notation $\begin{split}(\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{0}})_{0}&=-2\hat{L}^{0}(\mathbf{0})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{3}|A_{j}|^{2}\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{-i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{0},\vartheta)=:3\nu_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{3}|A_{j}|^{2},\\
(\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell}})_{0}&=-(2-\delta_{j\ell})\hat{L}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell})^{-1}\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{\ell}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell},\vartheta)=:\nu_{\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell}}A_{j}A_{\ell}\ \text{for }d(\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell},0)=2.\end{split}$ We now consider Fourier modes $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{0}$ , where it holds that $\begin{split}(I-\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))((\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{k}_{j}}+\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{2}(W_{mc}+\Psi;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta))&=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})\end{split}$ for the less-central contributions and $\begin{split}&\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{\Xi}(\tilde{A}_{j,+}(\Xi)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\tilde{A}_{j,-}(\Xi)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\\
&\qquad=\varepsilon(\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})(\tilde{A}_{j,+}(\Xi)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\tilde{A}_{j,-}(\Xi)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\\
&\qquad\quad+\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{2}(W_{mc}+\Psi;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)+\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{3}(W_{mc}+\Psi;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4}),\end{split}$ (3.24) We start by discussing the first equation. For this, we note that due to the resonance of the hexagonal lattice, there are quadratic interactions of the more-central modes that lie again at a Fourier mode in $\Gamma_{0}$ . However, we assume that these nonlinear terms gain an additional order of $\varepsilon$ , see Assumption B . Therefore, we find that $(I-\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon})\tilde{\Psi}_{k_{j}}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ , and hence, it is irrelevant for the leading-order equation on the centre manifold. Finally, we derive an equation for the more-central modes. To systematically exploit the Jordan-block structure at $\varepsilon=0$ , we introduce the following basis for the span of $\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}$ given by $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\dfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\bigr).$ (3.25) In particular, we point out that $(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))$ converges as $\varepsilon\to 0$ to $(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))$ where $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is the eigenvector corresponding to the zero eigenvalue of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}$ with first component normalised to one and $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is a generalised eigenvector to the zero eigenvalue with first component equal to zero. We then write $\tilde{A}_{j,+}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\tilde{A}_{j,-}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\tilde{A}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\varepsilon\tilde{B}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ (3.26) and note that the transformation $(\tilde{A}_{j,+},\tilde{A}_{j,-})\mapsto(\tilde{A}_{j},\tilde{B}_{j})$ is uniformly bounded for all $\varepsilon>0$ . Moreover, we also recover $\tilde{A}_{j,+}+\tilde{A}_{j,-}=\tilde{A}_{j}$ . We now derive evolution equations for $\tilde{A}_{j}$ and $\tilde{B}_{j}$ by projecting ( 3.24 ) onto $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ and $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , respectively. For this, we first note that $\begin{split}&(\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})(\tilde{A}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\varepsilon\tilde{B}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\\
&\quad=\dfrac{1}{2}(\lambda_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\lambda_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\tilde{A}_{j}+\varepsilon\dfrac{1}{2\varepsilon}(\lambda_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-\lambda_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\tilde{B}_{j}\\
&\quad=\Big(\dfrac{1}{2}(\lambda_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\lambda_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\dfrac{\varepsilon}{2}(\lambda_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-\lambda_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\Big)\tilde{A}_{j}\\
&\qquad+\Big(\dfrac{1}{2}(\lambda_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-\lambda_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\dfrac{\varepsilon}{2}(\lambda_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\lambda_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\Big)\tilde{B}_{j}\\
&\quad=\varepsilon\Big(-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}+\dfrac{\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j}\Big)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\\
&\qquad+\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\dfrac{\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j}\Big)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\end{split}$ where we use ( 3.25 ) and that the more-central eigenvalues are given in Proposition 2.7 . For the nonlinearity, we note as before that $\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{2}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})$ using Assumption B and $\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{3}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})$ . Hence, projecting on $\varphi_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ and $\varphi_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ we obtain that the dynamics on the centre manifold are described to leading order by the system $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}\tilde{A}_{j}&=-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}+\dfrac{\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j},\\
\partial_{\Xi}\tilde{B}_{j}&=\dfrac{\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j}+\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{mc},j}(\tilde{A}_{1},\tilde{A}_{2},\tilde{A}_{3},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{1},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{2},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{3}).\end{split}$ Here, $\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{mc},j}$ denotes the $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})$ -contributions of the nonlinearity. Note that these only depend on $A_{j}$ , see ( 3.23 ). Before determining the nonlinearities explicitly, we make the final coordinate change $A_{j}=\tilde{A}_{j}\quad B_{j}=-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}+\dfrac{\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j},$ cf. [ EW91 ] which transforms this system to $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{j}&=B_{j},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{j}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}A_{j}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}B_{j}+\dfrac{\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{mc},j}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},\bar{A}_{1},\bar{A}_{2},\bar{A}_{3}).\end{split}$ (3.27) We point out that this second transformation is effectively ’normalising’ the eigenvector $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ so that $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\varepsilon}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ . It remains to derive an expression for the nonlinear terms. Since we have already determined the $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ -terms of $\tilde{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ , we obtain the nonlinear terms by balancing at $\varepsilon^{3}e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}}$ . For this, the relevant contributions are quadratic combinations $A_{\ell_{1}},A_{\ell_{2}}$ such that $\mathbf{k}_{\ell_{1}}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell_{2}}=\mathbf{k}_{j}$ , quadratic combinations of $A_{\ell}$ and $\tilde{\Psi}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}$ with $\mathbf{k}_{\ell}+\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mathbf{k}_{j}$ , and cubic combinations of $A_{\ell_{1}},A_{\ell_{2}},A_{\ell_{3}}$ such that $\mathbf{k}_{\ell_{1}}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell_{2}}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell_{3}}=\mathbf{k}_{j}$ . In addition, to determine the normalisation of the projection $\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , we recall that the projection is explicitly given by $\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})W=\dfrac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot W}{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\dfrac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot W}{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),$ (3.28) where $\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ are the eigenvectors of the adjoint matrix $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})^{\ast}$ corresponding to the eigenvalue $\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}$ , which are normalised such that the last component is equal to one. A direct computation yields that $\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})_{i}=\sum_{j=i+1}^{4}\bar{a}_{j}(\bar{\lambda}_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{j-i-1},$ where we have set $a_{4}=-1$ , and so $\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})&=-\sum_{i=0}^{3}(\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{i}\sum_{j=i+1}^{4}a_{j}(\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{j-i-1}\\
&=-\sum_{k=0}^{4}ka_{k}(\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{k-1}=\mp\varepsilon\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),\end{split}$ (3.29) see [ EW91 ] . Next, we write the projection $\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ in the new basis $(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))$ , which yields $\begin{split}\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\mathcal{N}}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)&=\left(\dfrac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\mathcal{N}}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)}{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})}+\dfrac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\mathcal{N}}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)}{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})}\right)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\\
&\quad+\varepsilon\left(\dfrac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\mathcal{N}}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)}{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{+}(\mathbf{k}_{j})}-\dfrac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\mathcal{N}}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)}{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon,\ast}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{j})}\right)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\\
&=\alpha_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{N}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-\dfrac{2+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\mu_{0}}}\hat{N}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\end{split}$ where $\alpha_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\mathcal{O}(1)$ and we used that $\hat{\mathcal{N}}=(0,0,0,\hat{N})$ and the last component of $\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\pm}^{\varepsilon,\ast}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is normalised to one. Again, we notice that $\hat{N}(W_{mc}+\Psi(W_{mc});\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})$ and therefore, the nonlinearity only adds higher-order terms to the $A_{j}$ -equation. For the $B_{j}$ -equation, we find with that the usual combinatorics, see e.g. [ HJ25 ] for details, yields the nonlinear terms of order $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})$ $\begin{split}&\dfrac{\sqrt{c_{0}^{2}-16(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}\mu_{0}}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{mc},1}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},\bar{A}_{1},\bar{A}_{2},\bar{A}_{3})\\
&=-\dfrac{\varepsilon^{-3}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}\hat{N}(W_{mc}+\Psi(W_{mc});\mathbf{k}_{1},\vartheta)=-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}A_{1}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2})A_{1}).\end{split}$ (3.30) Here, the quadratic coefficient is given as $\begin{split}\beta_{2}:=2\frac{N_{2}(e^{-i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{-i\mathbf{k}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1},\vartheta)}{\varepsilon},\end{split}$ which is of order one for $\vartheta\in\Theta_{0}$ due to Assumption B . The self-interaction and the cross-interaction coefficients are given as $\begin{split}K_{0}&:=2\bigl[\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i\mathbf{0}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1},\vartheta)\nu_{0}+\hat{N}_{2}(e^{2i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{-i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1},\vartheta)\nu_{2\mathbf{k}_{1}}\bigr]+3\hat{N}_{3}(e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{-i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1},\vartheta),\\
K_{2}&:=2\bigl[\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i\mathbf{0}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1},\vartheta)\nu_{0}+\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1},\vartheta)\nu_{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{k}_{2}}\bigr]+6\hat{N}_{3}(e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{-i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1},\vartheta).\end{split}$ (3.31) Here, we recall that $\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{k}_{j}}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ and therefore, there are no quadratic combinations of $\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{k}_{j}}$ and $A_{\ell}$ with $\mathbf{k}_{j}+\mathbf{k}_{\ell}=\mathbf{k}_{1}$ at leading order. While we have only derived the leading-order term at $\mathbf{k}_{1}$ , we may obtain the leading-order terms at $\mathbf{k}_{2}$ and $\mathbf{k}_{3}$ by cyclic permutation of the $\mathbf{k}_{i}$ . We point out that the coefficients are the same in all equations due to the rotational invariance of the nonlinearity and are all real-valued due to the reflectional invariance of the nonlinearity, cf. Assumption A . Combining equations ( 3.27 ) and ( 3.30 ), we obtain the reduced equations on the centre manifold $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}&=B_{1},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{1}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}A_{1}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}B_{1}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}&=B_{2},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{2}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}A_{2}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}B_{2}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}&=B_{3},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{3}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}A_{3}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}B_{3}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\end{split}$ (3.32) which can also be written as the system of second-order equations $\begin{split}4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{1}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}+\mu_{0}A_{1}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{2}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}+\mu_{0}A_{2}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{3}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}+\mu_{0}A_{3}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0.\\
\end{split}$ (3.33) Remark 3.12. We note that the quadratic, self-interaction and cross-interaction coefficients are the same as in [ HJ25 ] . In addition, they are also consistent with [ Doe+03 ] by noting that in this paper, the quadratic nonlinearity is assumed to be of higher order, while in this paper, we only assume that the quadratic nonlinearity is small at the critical Fourier modes, cf. Assumption B . Remark 3.13. As in [ HJ25 , Rem. 4.5] we highlight that the higher-order terms in the $A_{1}$ -equation $\eqref{eq:reduced-equations-second-order}_{1}$ are of the form $A_{1}p_{1}(|A_{1}|^{2},|A_{2}|^{2},|A_{3}|^{2},q)+\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}p_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2},|A_{2}|^{2},|A_{3}|^{2},q)$ with $q=A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}+\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}$ for polynomials $p_{j}(|A_{1}|^{2},|A_{2}|^{2},|A_{3}|^{2},q)=\mathcal{O}((|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}+q)^{2})$ , $j=1,2$ . This follows from the rotational invariance of the nonlinearity, cf. Assumption A , from translation invariance and the fact that $\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{3}=0$ , cf. [ BG83 , Prop. 3.1] . Remark 3.14. We point out that the reduced equations on the centre manifold ( 3.32 ) are the same as the ones obtained in equation ( 1.5 ) via the asymptotic expansion in physical variables ( 1.4 ) in the Swift–Hohenberg equation ( 1.1 ). This is, of course, not a coincidence. In fact, one can show that the leading-order systems are equivalent in the following sense. If $U$ is a solution to the physical system ( 1.1 ) of the form $U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{3}A_{j}(\varepsilon\xi)e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}}+c.c.+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),$ one obtains a solution to the reduced equation on the centre manifold via $W=(U,(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{p})U,(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{p})^{2}U,(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{p})^{3}U)$ . Indeed, one observes that $W=\varepsilon W_{mc}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ with $W_{mc}\in\mathcal{Z}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}$ since $\xi$ -derivatives yield additional powers of $\varepsilon$ and $(1,(\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{p}),(\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{p})^{2},(\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{p})^{3})e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}}=(1,(i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}),(i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2},(i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{3})e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}}=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).$ On the other hand, solutions $(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},B_{1},B_{2},B_{3})$ to the reduced equations ( 3.32 ) yield solutions $W$ to the spatial-dynamics system via $W(\xi,\mathbf{p})=\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\tilde{A}_{j,+}(\varepsilon\xi)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\tilde{A}_{j,-}(\varepsilon\xi)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}}+c.c.+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),$ see Theorem 3.10 , where $\tilde{A}_{j,\pm}$ is obtained through ( 3.26 ). Finally, we obtain a solution to the physical system by restricting the first component and note that $\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})_{1}=1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ which yields $A_{j}=\tilde{A}_{j,+}+\tilde{A}_{j,-}$ , cf. [ EW91 , Hil20 ] . 3.5  The centre manifold reduction and reduced equations for $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ We now consider the case that $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , where $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})$ has a Jordan block of size four instead of size two, cf. Proposition 2.3 . For $\varepsilon>0$ this Jordan block then splits into three less-central eigenvalues with real part of order $\varepsilon^{1/3}$ and one more-central eigenvalue of the form $\lambda_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})=-\varepsilon\tfrac{\mu_{0}}{c_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ , cf. Proposition 2.7 . This makes it more complicated to separate the more-central and less-central eigenvalues. Indeed, Lemma 3.3 and Remark 3.4 demonstrate that the relevant projection $\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ behaves like $\varepsilon^{-1}$ . Therefore, we need to modify the centre manifold construction above. For this, we introduce the maps $\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})W&=(\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon,*}(\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdot W)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}(\mathbf{k}_{2}),\\
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})W&=(\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon,*}(\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdot W)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2}),\end{split}$ (3.34) where $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ is the eigenvector of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ corresponding to the more-central eigenvalue $\lambda_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ , $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ are the eigenvectors of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ corresponding to the less-central eigenvalues $\lambda_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ , and $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon,\ast}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ and $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon,\ast}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ are the eigenvectors of the corresponding adjoint eigenvalue problems. As above, $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ and $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ are normalised such that their first component equals one, and $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon,\ast}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ and $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon,\ast}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ are normalised such that their last component equals one. We point out that $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ and $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ are not projections. In particular, the more-central map satisfies $\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})W=(\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon,*}(\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}(\mathbf{k}_{2}))\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})W=-\varepsilon c_{0}\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})W,\end{split}$ where we used ( 3.29 ) and $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2}=0$ . We note that $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ and $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ map onto the more-central and less-central eigenspaces, respectively, and, in particular, are uniformly bounded for $\varepsilon>0$ . Using these maps, we define, similarly to ( 3.6 ), $\hat{W}_{mc}(\mathbf{k}_{2}):=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})\hat{W}(\mathbf{k}_{2}),\quad\hat{W}_{lc,j}(\mathbf{k}_{2}):=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})\hat{W}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ and introduce the rescaled variables $\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{mc}(\mathbf{k}_{2})=\varepsilon^{-\beta}\hat{W}_{mc}(\mathbf{k}_{2}),\quad\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{lc,j}(\mathbf{k}_{2})=\varepsilon^{-\gamma}\hat{W}_{lc,j}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ for $j=1,2,3$ and $0<\beta<1<\gamma<2$ as in ( 3.7 ) with explicit values given in ( 3.20 ). We then obtain the spatial-dynamics system $\begin{split}\partial_{\xi}\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{mc}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})&=\lambda_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{mc}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})+\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{\mathcal{N}}}_{mc}(\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{mc},\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{lc}+\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{h};\pm\mathbf{k}_{2},\vartheta),\\
\partial_{\xi}\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{lc,j}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})&=\lambda_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{lc,j}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})+\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{\mathcal{N}}}_{lc,j}(\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{mc},\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{lc}+\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{h};\pm\mathbf{k}_{2},\vartheta),\end{split}$ (3.35) with $j=1,2,3$ , at $\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mathbf{k}_{2}$ combined with system ( 3.8 ) for all other modes $\boldsymbol{\gamma}\neq\mathbf{k}_{2}$ . Here, $\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{\mathcal{N}}}_{mc}$ and $\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{\mathcal{N}}}_{lc,j}$ are defined like the rescaled nonlinearities after system ( 3.8 ) replacing the projections by $\tilde{P}^{\varepsilon}_{mc}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})$ and $\tilde{P}^{\varepsilon}_{lc,j}(\pm\mathbf{k}_{2})$ , respectively. Then, repeating the normal-form analysis of Section 3.2 and the proof of the centre manifold theorem in Section 3.3 line by line, using that $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ and $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{lc,j}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ are uniformly bounded for $\varepsilon>0$ and that the semigroup bounds for ( 3.35 ) are better than for the rest of the more-central and less-central spectrum (cf. Lemma 3.8 ), we obtain the following result. Theorem 3.15. Let $\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N}$ and $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . For any $\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{4})$ there exists an $\varepsilon_{0}>0$ such that for every $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ there exists a neighbourhood $O_{mc}^{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}$ of the origin and a map ${}^{\varepsilon}:O_{mc}^{\varepsilon}\to(I-\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon})\mathcal{Z}$ , which is at least quadratic, such that the following holds. (a) The neighbourhood $O_{mc}^{\varepsilon}$ is of size $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3/4+\delta})$ . (b) The centre manifold $\mathcal{M}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}=\{W=W_{mc}+{}^{\varepsilon}(W_{mc})\,:\,W_{mc}\in O_{mc}^{\varepsilon}\}$ contains all small bounded solutions to ( 1.11 ). (c) Solutions to the reduced equation $\partial_{\xi}W_{mc}=\mathcal{L}^{\varepsilon}W_{mc}+\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{N}(W_{mc}+{}^{\varepsilon}(W_{mc});\vartheta)$ (3.36) give rise to solutions $W$ to the full system ( 1.11 ) via $W=W_{mc}+{}^{\varepsilon}(W_{mc})$ . (d) The symmetries of the system ( 1.1 ) are preserved by the reduction function $\Psi$ . Finally, we derive the reduced equation on the centre manifold. For this, we make the modified ansatz $\begin{split}W=W_{mc}+\Psi(W_{mc})&=\varepsilon\sum_{j\in\{1,3\}}(\tilde{A}_{j,+}(\Xi)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\tilde{A}_{j,-}(\Xi)\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}}+\varepsilon A_{2}(\Xi)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})e^{i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}}+c.c.\\
&\quad+\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{A}_{1,\pm},A_{2},\tilde{A}_{3,\pm};\varepsilon),\end{split}$ with $\Xi=\varepsilon\xi$ . We then obtain an equation for $A_{2}$ by inserting the ansatz into ( 3.36 ) and applying the map $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\varepsilon}_{mc}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ . This yields $-\varepsilon^{3}c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}=-\varepsilon^{2}c_{0}\lambda_{\perp}(\mathbf{k}_{2})A_{2}+\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{mc},2}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},\bar{A}_{1},\bar{A}_{2},\bar{A}_{3})$ with $A_{1}=A_{1,+}+A_{1,-}$ and $A_{3}=A_{3,+}+A_{3,-}$ as in Section 3.4 . To determine the nonlinearity, we note that $\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}\hat{\mathcal{N}}(W;\mathbf{k}_{2},\vartheta)=\hat{N}(W;\mathbf{k}_{2},\vartheta)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2}),$ where we use again that $\hat{\mathcal{N}}=(0,0,0,\hat{N})$ and that the last component of $\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\perp}^{\varepsilon,*}(\mathbf{k}_{2})$ is normalised to one. Recalling that $\lambda_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{2})=-\varepsilon\tfrac{\mu_{0}}{c_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ , see Item 2.7(b) , and repeating the derivation of the equations for $A_{1}$ and $A_{3}$ as in Section 3.4 , we find $\begin{split}3\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{1}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}+\mu_{0}A_{1}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}+\mu_{0}A_{2}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
3\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{3}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}+\mu_{0}A_{3}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\end{split}$ (3.37) since $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1}=\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ and $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3}=-\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ . Note that ( 3.37 ) is consistent with ( 3.33 ). Indeed, we make this connection rigorous through a fast-slow analysis, see Section 4.4 . 3.6  The reduced equations for $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ In Section 3.4 , we assumed that $c_{0}\neq c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ for all $j=1,2,3$ , which yields that the more-central eigenvalues split at order $\varepsilon$ . We now discuss the case that $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ for one $j\in\{1,2,3\}$ . Since the eigenvalues $\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ are now equal at leading order, we have to modify the derivation of the reduced equation for $A_{j}$ and $B_{j}$ . For this, we recall that $\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{\pm}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ are the eigenvectors of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ corresponding to $\lambda_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ and we introduce the new basis $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\dfrac{1}{2}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\dfrac{1}{2\varepsilon^{3/2}}\bigl(\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\bigr)$ similar to ( 3.25 ). Using the expansion of the eigenvalues provided in Proposition 2.7 , we again find that $(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))$ converges to $(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))$ , where $\varphi_{+}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is the eigenvector corresponding to the zero eigenvalue of $\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ with first component normalised to one and $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{0}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ is a generalised eigenvalue with first component equal to zero. As in the non-critical case, we then write $\tilde{A}_{j,+}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\tilde{A}_{j,-}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\tilde{A}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\varepsilon^{3/2}\tilde{B}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),$ which defines a bounded transformation $(\tilde{A}_{j,+},\tilde{A}_{j,-})\to(\tilde{A}_{j},\tilde{B}_{j})$ . Then the linear terms are given by $\begin{split}&(\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-i\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})(\tilde{A}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\varepsilon^{3/2}\tilde{B}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))\\
&\quad=\varepsilon\Big(-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\frac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j}\Big)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\varepsilon^{\tfrac{5}{2}}\Big(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\frac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j}\Big)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\end{split}$ where we used that $\lambda_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})+\lambda_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=-\varepsilon\tfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})}$ and $\lambda_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-\lambda_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\varepsilon^{3/2}\tfrac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}$ . Combining the linear and nonlinear terms, we therefore find that $\begin{split}\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{\Xi}\tilde{A}_{j}&=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\frac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})\\
\varepsilon^{\tfrac{7}{2}}\partial_{\Xi}\tilde{B}_{j}&=\varepsilon^{\tfrac{7}{2}}\Big(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\frac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j}\Big)+\varepsilon^{3}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{mc},j}(\tilde{A}_{1},\tilde{A}_{2},\tilde{A}_{3},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{1},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{2},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{3}).\end{split}$ In particular, we find that the $\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ -contribution of the projected nonlinearity is of order $\varepsilon^{3}$ since $\hat{N}(W;k_{j},\vartheta)=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})$ . This is due to the computations $\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\pm}^{\varepsilon,\ast}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{\pm}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\mp\varepsilon^{\tfrac{3}{2}}\dfrac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ and therefore $\begin{split}\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\mathcal{N}}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)=\alpha_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{N}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})-\dfrac{1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\left(\dfrac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\right)^{-1}\hat{N}(W;\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{-}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j}),\end{split}$ with $\alpha_{+}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=\mathcal{O}(1)$ . We now proceed as in Section 3.4 and introduce the transformation $A_{j}=\tilde{A}_{j},\quad B_{j}=-\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{A}_{j}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\frac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\tilde{B}_{j}.$ Notably, $B_{j}$ is of the same order as $\partial_{\Xi}A_{j}$ since the $\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ in the $\tilde{B}_{j}$ -coefficient compensates for the different $\varepsilon$ -behaviour in the definition of $\tilde{B}_{j}$ . Using this transformation, we then find $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{j}&=B_{j}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{j}&=\varepsilon\Bigl(\frac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\Bigr)^{2}A_{j}-\Bigl(\dfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\Bigr)^{2}A_{j}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}B_{j}+\left(\dfrac{i^{3/2}\mu_{0}^{3/4}}{2\sqrt{2}(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\right)\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{mc},j}(\tilde{A}_{1},\tilde{A}_{2},\tilde{A}_{3},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{1},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{2},\bar{\tilde{A}}_{3}).\end{split}$ As before, $\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{mc},j}$ denotes the $\varepsilon^{3}$ -contributions of the (projected) nonlinearity. Since the combinatorics for the nonlinear terms are not affected by the value of $c_{0}$ , and using that $\bigl(\tfrac{c_{0}}{8(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}\bigr)^{2}=\tfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}}$ for $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , the reduced equations are given by ( 3.32 ) or, equivalently, ( 3.33 ). Remark 3.16. The calculation above shows that, although the algebraic manipulations are different, the reduced equations on the centre manifold are, to leading order, the same for $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ and $c_{0}\neq c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ . Indeed, this is not surprising since the reduced equations can also be obtained from the amplitude equations ( 1.5 ) by inserting a travelling-wave ansatz. Since the amplitude equations are derived in the stationary frame, they are independent of the chosen speed for the travelling wave. However, it turns out that the critical speed is still hidden in the dynamics of the reduced equations, which change when $c_{0}$ passes through $c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , see Section 4 and Figure 8 . Indeed, we observe that fronts at supercritical speeds have a monotone profile, while at subcritical speeds, fronts have an oscillatory leading edge. This is consistent with monostable fronts in reaction-diffusion systems. Remark 3.17. We only need to consider the case $c_{0}=c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ for $\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j}\neq 0$ . Otherwise, we find that $c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})=0$ , however, we have assumed that $c_{0}>0$ . 4  Analysis of reduced equations and pattern interfaces We now discuss the dynamics of the reduced equations. Specifically, we consider the existence of equilibria, their stability, and the existence of heteroclinic orbits since these correspond to planar patterns and pattern interfaces in the full system ( 1.1 ), respectively. The general strategy is to first analyse the leading-order dynamics given by the truncated system for $\theta\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{6})$ , which reads as $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}&=B_{1},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{1}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}A_{1}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}B_{1}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1}),\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}&=B_{2},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{2}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}A_{2}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}B_{2}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2}),\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}&=B_{3},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{3}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}A_{3}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}B_{3}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3}),\\
\end{split}$ (4.1) and is obtained by setting $\varepsilon=0$ in ( 3.32 ). Notably, this system is the travelling-wave ODE corresponding to the Ginzburg–Landau system obtained as a formal amplitude system close to a Turing instability, see ( 1.5 ). In the analysis, we restrict to real-valued solutions since these are sufficient to describe the dynamics typically observed in experiments. We then show the existence of equilibria in ( 4.1 ) corresponding to roll waves, hexagons and mixed modes in ( 1.1 ), cf. Corollary 4.1 . We note that, as already noticed in equivariant bifurcation theory, these are all possible real-valued equilibria. To analyse the stability of the equilibria, we show that the stability structure of the linearisation in the spatial system ( 4.1 ) can be characterised through the temporal stability of the corresponding equilibria in a system of Landau equations which governs the dynamics of spatially homogeneous solutions to the Ginzburg–Landau system ( 1.5 ). Specifically, if an equilibrium is temporally stable, the corresponding equilibrium in the spatial system is a saddle with even splitting between stable and unstable directions. Moreover, a temporally-unstable equilibrium corresponds to a stable equilibrium in the spatial system. Therefore, understanding a three-dimensional eigenvalue problem is sufficient to determine the stability structure in the six-dimensional spatial system. Subsequently, we discuss the existence of heteroclinic orbits between equilibria analytically. Outside of the invariant subspace of roll waves $\{A_{1},B_{1}\in\mathbb{R},A_{2}=B_{2}=A_{3}=B_{3}=0\}$ , where the leading-order system ( 4.1 ) reduces to a damped Duffing oscillator, this requires understanding the dynamics in the full six-dimensional phase space. Exploiting that the leading-order system ( 4.1 ) has a strictly decreasing Lyapunov function, for $\mu_{0}>0$ , we can rigorously show the existence of heteroclinic orbits connecting the non-trivial equilibrium with the lowest energy (either down-hexagons or roll waves) to the trivial equilibrium, which persist in the reduced equations ( 3.32 ) for $\varepsilon>0$ . In the pattern-forming system ( 1.1 ), these correspond to pattern interfaces, where the spatially homogeneous state is invaded by a planar pattern. Using fast-slow analysis, we then show that these orbits also exist in the reduced system for $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , which can be interpreted as the slow subsystem of ( 3.32 ) as $\theta\to\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . 4.1  Equilibria and their stability 4.1.1 Equilibria First, we consider the equilibria of the reduced equations ( 4.1 ) and their stability. We point out that the existence of equilibria does not depend on the direction of the front since equilibria of the reduced equations correspond to steady patterns in the Swift–Hohenberg equation ( 1.1 ), which is rotationally invariant. In particular, the results also apply to the leading-order system of the reduced equations ( 3.37 ), when $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Therefore, equilibria are given as solutions to $\begin{split}\mu_{0}A_{1}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1}&=0,\\
\mu_{0}A_{2}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2}&=0,\\
\mu_{0}A_{3}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3}&=0,\end{split}$ (4.2) with $A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\in\mathbb{C}$ . The system of equations ( 4.2 ) has been well-studied in the literature on equivariant bifurcation theory, and we refer to [ BG83 , GSK84 , Hoy07 ] for a full characterisation of solutions to reduced equations obtained from a hexagonal symmetry with translation invariance. In the context of this paper, we note that ( 4.2 ) is cubic to leading order, with small higher-order terms. Therefore, we can follow the discussion in [ Hoy07 , Sec. 5.4] on cubic truncations, and we find that, to leading order, the system ( 4.2 ) has only (a) the trivial solution $A_{1}=A_{2}=A_{3}=0$ ; (b) roll waves with $|A_{1}|>0$ and $A_{2}=A_{3}=0$ ; (c) hexagons with $A_{1}=A_{2}=A_{3}\neq 0$ ; (d) mixed modes (or rectangles) with $A_{2}=A_{3}$ and $A_{2}\neq A_{1}\neq 0$ as well as solutions of the same form obtained from $D_{6}$ symmetry. A simple calculation shows that no solution satisfies $|A_{2}|<|A_{1}|<|A_{3}|$ . These solutions can be calculated explicitly, see e.g. [ HJ25 ] and we summarise the results in the following corollary. Corollary 4.1. The equation ( 4.2 ) has the following solutions: (a) The trivial solution $\mathbf{A}_{T}:=(A_{1,T},A_{2,T},A_{3,T})=(0,0,0).$ (b) Roll waves $\mathbf{A}_{R}=(A_{1,R},A_{2,R},A_{3,R})=\Big(\pm\sqrt{-\frac{\mu_{0}}{K_{0}}},0,0\Big)$ (4.3) if $\mu_{0}K_{0}<0$ . (c) Hexagons $\begin{split}\mathbf{A}_{H_{\pm}}&:=(A_{1,H_{\pm}},A_{2,H_{\pm}},A_{3,H_{\pm}})\\
&=\Big(\frac{-\beta_{2}\mp\sqrt{\beta_{2}^{2}-4\mu_{0}(K_{0}+2K_{2})}}{2(K_{0}+2K_{2})},\frac{-\beta_{2}\mp\sqrt{\beta_{2}^{2}-4\mu_{0}(K_{0}+2K_{2})}}{2(K_{0}+2K_{2})},\frac{-\beta_{2}\mp\sqrt{\beta_{2}^{2}-4\mu_{0}(K_{0}+2K_{2})}}{2(K_{0}+2K_{2})}\Big)\end{split}$ (4.4) if $K_{0}+2K_{2}\neq 0$ and $\beta_{2}^{2}-4\mu_{0}(K_{0}+2K_{2})>0$ . If $A_{1,H_{\pm}}>0$ the solutions are called up-hexagons and if $A_{1,H_{\pm}}<0$ , they are called down-hexagons . (d) Mixed modes and false hexagons $\begin{split}\mathbf{A}_{M\!M}&:=(A_{1,M\!M},A_{2,M\!M},A_{3,M\!M})\\
&=\Big(\frac{\beta_{2}}{K_{0}-K_{2}},\sqrt{-\frac{K_{0}\beta_{2}^{2}+(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}\mu_{0}}{(K_{0}+K_{2})(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}}},\sqrt{-\frac{K_{0}\beta_{2}^{2}+(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}\mu_{0}}{(K_{0}+K_{2})(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}}}\Big)\end{split}$ if $\tfrac{K_{0}\beta_{2}^{2}+(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}\mu_{0}}{K_{0}+K_{2}}<0$ . In addition, if $-\tfrac{K_{0}\beta_{2}^{2}}{(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}}<\mu_{0}<-\tfrac{\beta_{2}^{2}(2K_{0}+K_{2})}{(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}}$ these solutions are called mixed modes . For larger $\mu_{0}$ they are called false hexagons . Remark 4.2. Note that the equilibria of the reduced equations ( 3.19 ) and ( 3.36 ) correspond to steady patterns of the Swift–Hohenberg equation ( 1.1 ) which are, to leading order, given by $u(t,x)=\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{3}A_{j}e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}}+c.c.+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})=\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{3}2A_{j}\cos(\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),$ see Figure 7 . a roll waves b up-hexagons c down-hexagons d mixed modes e mixed modes f false hexagons Figure 7 : Depiction of all stationary patterns obtained in Corollary 4.1 . Panels ( 7d ) , ( 7e ) and ( 7f ) show different variations of patterns on the mixed modes and false hexagons branch for increasing values of $\mu_{0}$ . Remark 4.3. We point out that, due to translational and reflectional invariance, for any solution $(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})$ to ( 4.2 ), we find a corresponding solution given by $(A_{1},-A_{2},-A_{3})$ . This invariance is obtained by shifting by a full hexagon in $x_{2}$ -direction, which maps $\mathbf{k}_{3}\mapsto\mathbf{k}_{2}$ and $-\mathbf{k}_{2}\mapsto-\mathbf{k}_{3}$ , and then applying reflection symmetry along the $x_{2}$ -axis. 4.1.2 Temporal vs. spatial stability We now discuss the stability of the equilibria listed in Corollary 4.1 . For this, we introduce some notation. Let $\mathbf{A}=(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})$ be a solution to ( 4.2 ). Then, we denote the linearisation of the corresponding equilibrium $(A_{1},0,A_{2},0,A_{3},0)$ of the first-order spatial-dynamics system ( 3.32 ) by $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ . The eigenvalue structure of $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ is characterised by the spectral stability of the equilibrium as a solution to the system of Landau equations $\begin{split}\partial_{T}A_{1}&=\mu_{0}A_{1}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1},\\
\partial_{T}A_{2}&=\mu_{0}A_{2}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2},\\
\partial_{T}A_{3}&=\mu_{0}A_{3}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3},\end{split}$ (4.5) which is the system of amplitude equations ( 1.5 ) without the spatial derivatives. Note that since the (degenerate) diffusion operator in ( 1.5 ) is diagonal, spectral stability in ( 4.5 ) implies spectral stability in ( 1.5 ). Therefore, we refer to this as PDE stability . The following lemma then relates the PDE stability of an equilibrium to its stability in the leading-order system ( 4.1 ) on the centre manifold. This relation was already noted in [ Doe+03 ] , and we provide a proof for completeness. Lemma 4.4. Let $\theta\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{6})$ and $\mathbf{A}=(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an equilibrium of the spatial-dynamics system ( 4.1 ). If the linearisation of the Landau equations ( 4.5 ) has $n$ unstable eigenvalues with $0\leqslant n\leqslant 3$ and $3-n$ stable eigenvalues, the linearisation of the spatial-dynamics system $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ has $3+n$ stable eigenvalues and $3-n$ unstable eigenvalues. In particular, if $\mathbf{A}$ is PDE-stable, then $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ has three eigenvalues with negative real parts and three with positive real parts. Proof. We first note that $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ is an eigenvalue of $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ with eigenvector $\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in\mathbb{C}^{6}$ if and only if the quadratic matrix equation $(\mathcal{D}\lambda^{2}+c_{0}I\lambda+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A}))\boldsymbol{\psi}=0,$ (4.6) where the matrix $\mathcal{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}$ encodes the diffusion coefficients, i.e., $\mathcal{D}=\begin{pmatrix}4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}&0&0\\
0&4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}&0\\
0&0&4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}\end{pmatrix},$ and the matrix $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}$ is the linearisation of the Landau system ( 4.5 ) about the equilibrium $\mathbf{A}$ and given by $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})=\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{0}+3K_{0}A_{1}^{2}+K_{2}(A_{2}^{2}+A_{3}^{2})&\beta_{2}A_{3}+2K_{2}A_{1}A_{2}&\beta_{2}A_{2}+2K_{2}A_{1}A_{3}\\
\beta_{2}A_{3}+2K_{2}A_{1}A_{2}&\mu_{0}+3K_{0}A_{2}^{2}+K_{2}(A_{1}^{2}+A_{3}^{2})&\beta_{2}A_{1}+2K_{2}A_{2}A_{3}\\
\beta_{2}A_{2}+2K_{2}A_{1}A_{3}&\beta_{2}A_{1}+2K_{2}A_{2}A_{3}&\mu_{0}+3K_{0}A_{3}^{2}+K_{2}(A_{1}^{2}+A_{2}^{2})\end{pmatrix}.$ (4.7) Additionally, $\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}$ is related to $\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}$ via $\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
\lambda&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&\lambda&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\psi}.$ The equation ( 4.6 ) is known as a quadratic eigenvalue problem, see e.g. [ TM01 ] , and the equivalence to the spatial-dynamics eigenvalue problem follows with the same algebraic manipulations as in [ HJ25 , Lem. 5.3] . We first note that for $0<\theta<\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ , $\mathcal{D}$ is positive-definite. Next, we note some properties of the quadratic eigenvalue problem ( 4.6 ), which follow from results in [ TM01 ] . Since the matrix $\mathcal{D}$ is positive-definite, ( 4.6 ) is non-singular. In particular, this yields that ( 4.6 ) has six finite eigenvalues, as expected. Additionally, $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ is symmetric. Therefore, ( 4.6 ) is self-adjoint, and the eigenvalues $\lambda$ are either real-valued or appear in complex-conjugate pairs. Finally, multiplying ( 4.6 ) from the left by $\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}$ and normalising the eigenvector $\boldsymbol{\psi}$ such that $\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\boldsymbol{\psi}=1$ , we obtain that $\lambda$ satisfies the quadratic polynomial $\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\psi}\lambda^{2}+c_{0}\lambda+\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})\boldsymbol{\psi}=0,$ (4.8) where $c_{0}>0$ by assumption and $\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\psi}>0$ since $\mathcal{D}$ is positive-definite. We now prove both extreme cases of the lemma, i.e. if $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ has $n=0$ or $n=3$ unstable eigenvalues. In the former case ( $n=0$ ), we find that $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ is negative-definite and therefore $\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})\boldsymbol{\psi}<0$ . Therefore, solutions to the polynomial ( 4.8 ) come in pairs with positive and negative real parts. Using the equivalence of ( 4.6 ) to the linear eigenvalue problem for $\mathfrak{L}$ , we obtain that $\mathfrak{L}$ has three stable and three unstable eigenvalues. This proves the case $n=0$ . The case $n=3$ follows with the same arguments by noting that $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ is now positive-definite and thus $\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})\boldsymbol{\psi}>0$ , which yields that all solutions to ( 4.8 ) have negative real parts. Therefore, $\mathfrak{L}$ has six stable and no unstable eigenvalues. It thus remains to prove the intermediate cases $n=1,2$ where $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ has both stable and unstable eigenvalues and is thus indefinite. For this, we define the shifted quadratic eigenvalue problem $(\mathcal{D}\lambda^{2}+c_{0}\lambda I+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})+\kappa I)\boldsymbol{\psi}=0,$ (4.9) with $\kappa\in\mathbb{R}$ . Since $\kappa I$ shifts the eigenvalues of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ by $\kappa$ , we find that for $\kappa\ll 0$ , all eigenvalues of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ have negative real part ( $n=0$ ) while for $\kappa\gg 0$ all eigenvalues of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ have positive real part ( $n=3$ ). Therefore, ( 4.9 ) interpolates between the two extreme cases. Again, the solutions $\lambda$ to ( 4.9 ) are also solutions to $\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\psi}\lambda^{2}+c_{0}\lambda I+\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})\boldsymbol{\psi}+\kappa=0.$ (4.10) Note that there are at most three values of $\kappa$ where ( 4.10 ) has solutions on the imaginary axis, i.e. where at least one eigenvalue crosses from the right to the left complex half-plane as $\kappa$ increases through the critical value. Since $c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ , the explicit solution formula for ( 4.10 ) shows that ( 4.10 ) cannot have solutions in $i\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ . Therefore, all eigenvalues have to cross through $\lambda=0$ . Next, we note that ( 4.9 ) has an eigenvalue $\lambda=0$ if and only if $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}+\kappa I$ has an eigenvalue $\lambda=0$ . In this case, $\boldsymbol{\psi}$ is also an eigenvector of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}+\kappa I$ corresponding to the eigenvalue $\lambda=0$ . Therefore, as $\kappa$ increases, there is a one-to-one correspondence between eigenvalues of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}+\kappa I$ crossing from the right to the left complex half-plane and solutions to ( 4.9 ) crossing from the right to the left complex half-plane. Therefore, increasing $\kappa$ from $-\infty$ to $\kappa=0$ and counting the eigenvalues in $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}+\kappa I$ crossing the imaginary axis shows that if there are $n$ unstable eigenvalues of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}$ , there are $3+n$ stable eigenvalues of the quadratic eigenvalue problem ( 4.6 ), and, by equivalence, $3+n$ stable eigenvalues of $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ . This completes the proof.
∎ Remark 4.5. The proof of Lemma 4.4 shows that the assumption $c_{0}>0$ plays a crucial role for the eigenvalue-structure of $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ . In particular, in the case $c_{0}<0$ we find that the eigenvalues of $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ cross from stable to unstable (instead from unstable to stable) as $\mathbf{A}$ becomes PDE-unstable. In particular, if $\mathbf{A}$ is fully PDE-unstable, i.e. $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ has only unstable eigenvalues, then $(A_{1},0,A_{2},0,A_{3})$ is an unstable equilibrium in the leading-order system ( 4.1 ). Remark 4.6. Although Lemma 4.4 is formulated for the leading-order problem ( 4.1 ), we note that the result applies more generally to travelling-wave solutions in reaction-diffusion systems of the form $\partial_{t}u=\mathcal{D}\partial_{x}^{2}u+f(u)$ with $u(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$ , $\mathcal{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}$ and a smooth function $f:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}$ , for $m\in\mathbb{N}$ . Assume that $u^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$ is an equilibrium. The key ingredients to extend the proof of Lemma 4.4 are that the diffusion matrix $\mathcal{D}$ is positive-definite and that the linearisation about $u^{\ast}$ given by $\mathrm{D}f(u^{\ast})$ is symmetric. While the first condition is typically satisfied since it is equivalent to parabolicity of the system, the second condition is restrictive. Moreover, the result can fail without this symmetry condition, as the following example shows. Let $\mathcal{D}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{D}f(u^{\ast})=\dfrac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
3&1\end{pmatrix}.$ The eigenvalues of $Df(u^{\ast})$ are complex conjugate with real part $\tfrac{1}{2}$ . Therefore, $u^{\ast}$ is PDE-unstable, and we would expect that the linearisation of the corresponding spatial-dynamics system for travelling waves with speed $c=1$ has only stable eigenvalues. However, an explicit computation shows that the corresponding quadratic eigenvalue problem $(\mathcal{D}\lambda^{2}+I\lambda+\mathrm{D}f(u^{\ast}))\boldsymbol{\psi}$ has two complex-conjugate eigenvalues with negative real part and two complex-conjugate eigenvalues with positive real part. We point out that the different diffusion coefficients in this counter-example are necessary. Indeed, if $\mathcal{D}=\alpha I$ for $\alpha>0$ , then eigenvectors of the quadratic eigenvalues problem $(\mathcal{D}\lambda^{2}+I\lambda+\mathrm{D}f(u^{\ast}))\boldsymbol{\psi}$ and eigenvectors of $\mathrm{D}f(u^{\ast})$ coincide and the proof of Lemma 4.4 applies. Remark 4.7. Note that the mechanism obtained in Lemma 4.4 resembles the behaviour of spatial eigenvalues in Proposition 2.7 , where Fourier modes which are ’PDE-stable’ induce an even splitting of the spatial eigenvalues into stable and unstable eigenvalues for $\varepsilon>0$ , whereas the more-central eigenvalues connected to ’PDE-unstable’ Fourier modes all have negative real part for $\varepsilon>0$ . The mechanism for the more-central modes is indeed very similar, in fact, the quadratic polynomial for the $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ -terms of the more-central eigenvalues is the same as the quadratic eigenvalue problem obtained for the trivial equilibrium from Lemma 4.4 , cf. Remark 4.9 . However, the mechanism for the even splitting of the less-central modes is different and relies on the fact that the less-central eigenvalues emerge from a non-trivial point on the imaginary axis, cf. Appendix A . We now handle the case $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Here, we recall that the reduced equation on the centre manifold is given by ( 3.37 ) and consider its leading-order system. This can be rewritten as a five-dimensional first-order system for the unknowns $(A_{1},B_{1},A_{2},A_{3},B_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{5}$ . For an equilibrium $\mathbf{A}=(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})$ given in Corollary 4.1 we then denote, by a slight abuse of notation, the linearisation about the corresponding equilibrium $(A_{1},0,A_{2},A_{3},0)$ in the first-order spatial-dynamics system by $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})\in\mathbb{R}^{5\times 5}$ . Lemma 4.8. Let $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ and $\mathbf{A}=(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}$ be an equilibrium of the leading-order system to ( 3.37 ). If the linearisation of the Landau equations ( 4.5 ) has $n$ unstable eigenvalues with $0\leqslant n\leqslant 3$ and $3-n$ stable eigenvalues, the linearisation of the spatial-dynamics system $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ has $2+n$ stable eigenvalues and $3-n$ unstable eigenvalues. In particular, if $\mathbf{A}$ is PDE-stable, then $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ has three eigenvalues with negative and three eigenvalues with positive real part. Proof. By the same arguments as in Lemma 4.4 $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ is an eigenvalue of $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A})$ with eigenvector $\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in\mathbb{C}^{5}$ if and only if $(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\psi})$ is a solution to the quadratic eigenvalue problem ( 4.8 ) with $\mathcal{D}=\begin{pmatrix}3&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&3\end{pmatrix},$ and $\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}$ is related to $\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}$ via $\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
\lambda&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\psi}.$ Note that since $\mathcal{D}$ is singular, we cannot guarantee that $\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\ast}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\psi}>0$ for an eigenvector $\boldsymbol{\psi}$ . Thus, while the structure of the proof is similar to Lemma 4.4 , we need to modify the specific arguments. Computing $\deg\det(\mathcal{D}\lambda^{2}+c_{0}I\lambda+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A}))=5$ and following [ TM01 ] , there are five finite eigenvalues and one infinite eigenvalue. In the case $n=3$ in which $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ is positive-definite, we obtain again that solutions to ( 4.8 ) need to have negative real parts and there are five stable eigenvalues. If $n=0$ and $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ is negative-definite, we first transform the problem: there is an orthogonal matrix $S$ such that $S^{T}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})S=\Lambda$ where $\Lambda$ is diagonal and negative-definite. Applying this to ( 4.8 ), we obtain the quadratic matrix equation $(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\lambda^{2}+c_{0}I\lambda+\Lambda)\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0.$ (4.11) where $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=S^{T}\boldsymbol{\psi}$ and $\tilde{\mathcal{D}}=S^{T}\mathcal{D}S$ has rank two and is positive semidefinite and symmetric. With the same argument as in the proof of Lemma 4.4 , for any negative-definite matrix $\Lambda$ , ( 4.11 ) cannot have solutions on the imaginary axis. We now define the interpolation $\Lambda_{t}=-tI+(1-t)\Lambda$ , which is strictly contained in the set of negative-definite matrices. Hence, the number of eigenvalues with positive and negative real parts to ( 4.11 ) with $\Lambda$ replaced by $\Lambda_{t}$ is independent of $t$ , and so, we can also study $(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\lambda^{2}+c_{0}I\lambda-I)\check{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0.$ (4.12) Now, let $P_{\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}$ be the orthogonal projection onto the kernel of $\tilde{\mathcal{D}}$ and assume $(\lambda,\check{\boldsymbol{\psi}})$ is a solution to ( 4.12 ). If $P_{\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\neq 0$ , then applying $P_{\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}$ to ( 4.12 ) one obtains $0=\lambda^{2}P_{\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+(c_{0}\lambda-I)P_{\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=(c_{0}\lambda-I)P_{\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}},$ since the $\operatorname{image}(\tilde{\mathcal{D}})\perp\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}$ , using that $\tilde{\mathcal{D}}$ is symmetric. But this gives $\lambda=\tfrac{1}{c_{0}}>0$ and $\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0$ follows, so that $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}$ , which is one-dimensional, so that there is precisely one solution of this type. If $P_{\ker\tilde{\mathcal{D}}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0$ , we can argue as before that there are two eigenvalues for each eigenvector $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}$ , one with positive and one with negative real part. So there are three unstable and two stable eigenvalues. The general case $0<n<3$ now again follows by the same argument as in the proof of Lemma 4.4 .
∎ 4.1.3 Stability of equilibria As motivated above, Lemmas 4.4 and 4.8 allow for a simpler characterisation of the eigenvalue structure of the linearisation in the leading-order system ( 4.1 ). From this, we make the immediate observation that the eigenvalue structure does not depend on the direction $\mathbf{d}$ . To obtain more detailed information about the stability structure of the equilibria in Corollary 4.1 , we now consider their linearisation in the Landau system ( 4.5 ). We recall that the linearisation in the Landau system ( 4.5 ) about an equilibrium $\mathbf{A}$ , denoted as above by $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A})$ , is given by ( 4.7 ). First, we note that $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}(\mathbf{A}_{T})=\mu_{0}I$ is diagonal. Therefore, for $\mu_{0}<0$ , the corresponding equilibrium in the spatial-dynamics system is a saddle with 3 stable and 3 unstable eigenvalues, whereas for $\mu_{0}>0$ it is a stable equilibrium. More specifically, the quadratic eigenvalue problem decouples into three independent equations, given by $0=4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}\lambda^{2}+c_{0}\lambda+\mu_{0},\quad j=1,2,3.$ (4.13) This implies that the eigenvalues of $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A}_{T})$ are real for all $c_{0}>c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ . As $c_{0}$ decreases through $c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , two eigenvalues collide and then split into a pair of complex-conjugate eigenvalues. In particular, this changes the qualitative dynamics of orbits close to the trivial equilibrium. For $c_{0}>c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ for all $j=1,2,3$ , orbits decay into the trivial state monotonically, whereas for $c_{0}<c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j})$ , the trivial equilibrium is a stable centre and oscillations occur, see Figure 8 . This is well-known in the one-dimensional case, see e.g. [ Hil20 ] , and similar behaviour occurs for other monostable fronts, for example, in the Fisher–KPP equation [ SU17 , Sec. 7.1] . a Subcritical front speed b Critical front speed c Supercritical front speed Figure 8 : Numerical simulations of heteroclinic orbits between $(\mathbf{A}_{H_{+}},\mathbf{0})$ and $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ and the corresponding pattern interfaces connecting up-hexagons to the trivial state for increasing values of $c_{0}$ . ( 8a ) depicts the subcritical regime $c_{0}<\max_{j}(c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))$ and the orbit oscillates close to $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ which is a stable centre in this regime. ( 8b ) depicts the critical speed $c_{0}=\max_{j}(c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{j}))$ , where the front decays monotonically. Increasing $c$ into the supercritical regime leads to a less steep monotone front profile. This is consistent with monostable fronts in reaction-diffusion systems, where the critical front is the steepest, see [ AHS25 ] . Observe that, for illustration purposes, we have amplified the pattern interfaces close to the trivial state. Remark 4.9. We highlight that ( 4.13 ) recovers the leading-order equation for the $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)$ -contribution of the more-central eigenvalues in Proposition 2.7 , cf. ( 2.8 ). For the other equilibria, we note that these can be grouped into two cases: $\mathbf{A}=(A,A,A)$ with $A\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ and $\mathbf{A}=(A,B,B)$ with $A\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ and $B\in\mathbb{R}$ . The former group consists of hexagons, and the latter encompasses roll waves, mixed modes, and false hexagons. Thus, the corresponding linearisations have the general structure $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}((A,A,A))=\begin{pmatrix}a&b&b\\
b&a&b\\
b&b&a\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}((A,B,B))=\begin{pmatrix}a&e&e\\
e&b&f\\
e&f&b\end{pmatrix},$ with $a,b,e,f\in\mathbb{R}$ , respectively. From these general forms, we can directly obtain that the eigenvectors of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}((A,A,A))$ are given by $\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
-1\end{pmatrix},\text{ and }\begin{pmatrix}2\\
-1\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{with eigenvalues }a+2b,a-b,a-b$ and the eigenvectors of $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}((A,B,B))$ are given by $\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-\frac{2e}{\sqrt{(-a+b+f)^{2}+8e^{2}}-a+b+f}\\
-\frac{2e}{\sqrt{(-a+b+f)^{2}+8e^{2}}-a+b+f}\end{pmatrix},\text{ and }\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\sqrt{(-a+b+f)^{2}+8e^{2}}+a-b-f}{2e}\\
1\\
1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
-1\end{pmatrix}$ with eigenvalues $\tfrac{1}{2}\left(-\sqrt{(-a+b+f)^{2}+8e^{2}}+a+b+f\right)$ , $\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{(-a+b+f)^{2}+8e^{2}}+a+b+f\right)$ , and $b-f$ , which for roll waves reduces to the eigenvalues $a$ , $b-f$ , and $b+f$ . Recalling that we can choose $\beta_{2}>0$ without loss of generality and computing these eigenvalues for the different parameter ranges, we obtain the bifurcation diagrams shown in Figure 9 . a $K_{2}<K_{0}<0$ b $K_{0}<K_{2}<0$ Figure 9 : Two examples of bifurcation diagrams for stationary patterns on the hexagonal lattice including the dimension of the unstable manifold, which is calculated using the spectrum of the corresponding linearisation $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GL}}$ of the Landau equations ( 4.5 ). 4.2  Analytic construction of some heteroclinic orbits We recall from the beginning of this section that our goal is to construct heteroclinic orbits in the leading-order system ( 4.1 ), which correspond to planar pattern interfaces in the full problem ( 1.1 ). We note that since ( 4.1 ) has a six-dimensional phase space, understanding its global dynamics analytically is a non-trivial problem. Therefore, we present here selected orbits that can be constructed using well-understood analytical methods. While it might be possible to obtain a deeper understanding using topological methods such as Conley index theory, see e.g. [ MM02 ] , this is beyond the scope of this paper. We restrict the following analysis to the parameter set $\beta_{2}\geqslant 0$ , $K_{0}<0$ and $K_{2}<0$ , for which we obtain a bifurcation diagram as in Figure 9 and which is also found in [ Doe+03 ] where $\beta_{2}>0$ , $K_{0}=-3$ and $K_{2}=-6$ . Additionally, we restrict to the case $\theta\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{6})$ and note that the results can be extended to the case $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ using the fast-slow analysis presented in Section 4.4 . We first note that $\{A_{1},B_{1}\in\mathbb{R},A_{2}=B_{2}=A_{3}=B_{3}=0\}$ is an invariant subspace of the leading-order reduced equations ( 4.1 ), which contains roll waves. In fact, it is an invariant subspace including the higher-order terms in ( 3.32 ) due to Remark 3.13 . In this space, the reduced equations on the centre manifold simplify to $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}&=B_{1},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{1}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}A_{1}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}B_{1}-\dfrac{K_{0}A_{1}^{3}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}},\end{split}$ which is the first-order formulation of a homogeneous, damped Duffing oscillator. For this system, when $\mu_{0}>0$ , heteroclinic orbits connecting the non-trivial equilibrium to the trivial equilibrium can be constructed via a straightforward phase-plane analysis, see Figure 10 . a b Figure 10 : Phase plane of the damped Duffing oscillator for $\mu_{0}=1$ , $K_{0}=-1$ and $(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}=0.9$ . Here, ( 10a ) depicts the case of subcritical speed $c_{0}=0.8<c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{1})\approx 3.795$ , and ( 10b ) depicts the supercitical case $c_{0}=4>c_{\mathrm{crit}}(\mathbf{k}_{1})$ . While in the case $\mathbf{d}=(1,0)$ there is another invariant subspace $\{A_{2}=A_{3}\text{ and }B_{2}=B_{3}\}$ , this is not invariant for any other $\mathbf{d}$ with $\theta\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{6})$ since $(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}\neq(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}$ . Even in the case $\mathbf{d}=(1,0)$ , the phase space is still four-dimensional. Nevertheless, we are still able to construct selected heteroclinic orbits analytically. Since the construction works the same way, we directly consider the full leading-order system ( 4.1 ) for general directions $\mathbf{d}$ and prove that for any $c_{0}>0$ there exists a heteroclinic orbit connecting either down-hexagons $\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}}$ or roll waves $\mathbf{A}_{R}$ to the trivial state $\mathbf{A}_{T}$ depending on the parameter values $(\mu_{0},K_{0},K_{2})$ . The main observation for the analysis is that the leading-order system ( 4.1 ) has a Lyapunov function given by $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=\sum_{j=1}^{3}2(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{j})^{2}|B_{j}|^{2}+\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{3}|A_{j}|^{2}+\beta_{2}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}+\dfrac{K_{0}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{3}|A_{j}|^{4}+\dfrac{K_{2}}{2}(A_{1}^{2}A_{2}^{2}+A_{1}^{2}A_{3}^{2}+A_{2}^{2}A_{3}^{2}).$ (4.14) Indeed, a direct calculation gives that along a solution $(\mathbf{A}(\Xi),\mathbf{B}(\Xi))$ $\partial_{\Xi}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}(\Xi),\mathbf{B}(\Xi))=-c_{0}|\mathbf{B}(\Xi)|^{2}\leqslant 0.$ (4.15) From this, we note that for $c_{0}>0$ , the Lyapunov function $\mathcal{H}$ is strictly decreasing along non-stationary solutions. For $c_{0}=0$ , we find that $\mathcal{H}$ is conserved along solutions. In fact, the leading-order system ( 4.1 ) is a Hamiltonian system with Hamiltonian $\mathcal{H}$ . This observation can be used to show that there exist $\mu_{H_{+}\leftrightarrow T}<0$ and $\mu_{H_{+}\leftrightarrow R}>0$ and corresponding reversible heteroclinic orbits in ( 4.1 ) from $\mathbf{A}_{H_{+}}$ to $\mathbf{A}_{T}$ and $\mathbf{A}_{H_{+}}$ to $\mathbf{A}_{R}$ , respectively, using variational techniques for Hamiltonian systems, see e.g. [ Ste08 ] . However, we note that the centre manifold analysis in Theorem 3.10 does not apply for $c_{0}=0$ . While a spatial centre manifold approach, see e.g. [ HS12 ] , can be used to construct a centre manifold in the case $c_{0}=0$ , the resulting manifold can be of arbitrary dimension depending on the direction $\mathbf{d}$ . Although the leading-order system is expected to have an invariant manifold where the dynamics are given by ( 3.32 ), this does not extend to the full centre manifold. In particular, due to the presence of highly oscillatory higher-order terms, the persistence of the orbits is non-trivial. We plan to treat this problem in a future paper. Using the Lyapunov function, we now construct heteroclinic orbits for $\mu_{0}>0$ and $c_{0}>0$ . More specifically, the orbit connects the non-trivial equilibrium with the lowest energy, which is either down-hexagons $\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}}$ or roll waves $\mathbf{A}_{R}$ as shown below, to the trivial equilibrium. The construction uses the following strategy. Let $\mathbf{A}_{\ell}$ be the non-trivial equilibrium with the lowest energy. Then, we define the trapping region $\Omega$ as the connected subset of the sublevel set $\{(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\in\mathbb{R}^{6}\,:\,\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})<\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})\}$ that contains $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ and show that $\Omega$ is a bounded subset of the stable manifold of the trivial equilibrium. Note here that since $\mu_{0}>0$ , the trivial equilibrium is stable due to Lemma 4.4 . Thus, the corresponding stable manifold is six-dimensional. Moreover, we show that $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})\in\partial\Omega$ . To complete the construction, it remains to show that the unstable eigenspace of the equilibrium $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ intersects $\Omega$ . This yields the following result. Theorem 4.10. Let $\theta\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{6})$ , $c_{0}>0$ . (a) If $K_{2}<K_{0}$ , for every $\mu_{0}>0$ there exists a heteroclinic orbit $(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}\to T},\mathbf{B}_{H_{-}\to T})$ in ( 4.1 ) such that $\lim_{\Xi\to-\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}\to T},\mathbf{B}_{H_{-}\to T})=(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\Xi\to+\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}\to T},\mathbf{B}_{H_{-}\to T})=(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0}).$ (b) If $K_{2}\in(K_{0},0)$ there exists a $\mu_{1}>0$ , which is given by $\mu_{1}=-\frac{\beta_{2}^{2}K_{0}}{2\sqrt{2}\sqrt{K_{0}(K_{0}+K_{2})^{3}}-2K_{0}(K_{0}+3K_{2})}$ (4.16) such that for every $\mu_{0}\in(0,\mu_{1})$ there exists a heteroclinic orbit $(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}\to T},\mathbf{B}_{H_{-}\to T})$ as in 4.10(a) , and for $\mu_{0}>\mu_{1}$ there exists a heteroclinic orbit $(\mathbf{A}_{R\to T},\mathbf{B}_{R\to T})$ to ( 4.1 ) such that $\lim_{\Xi\to-\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{R\to T},\mathbf{B}_{R\to T})=(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\Xi\to+\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{R\to T},\mathbf{B}_{R\to T})=(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0}).$ a b c d Figure 11 : Logarithmic plot of energy levels for different parameter regimes.
Panels ( 11a ) and ( 11b ) correspond to
the case $K_{2}<K_{0}<0$ , with $K_{0}=-0.3$ and $K_{2}=-0.6$ .
Panels ( 11c ) and ( 11d ) correspond to
the case $K_{0}<K_{2}<-\tfrac{K_{0}}{2}$ , with $K_{0}=-1.2$ and $K_{2}=-0.6$ . Proof. For $\beta_{2}=0$ , the energies can be computed explicitly as $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{4K_{0}},\quad\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{\pm}},\mathbf{0})=-\dfrac{3\mu_{0}^{2}}{4(K_{0}+2K_{2})},\quad\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{M\!M},\mathbf{0})=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{2(K_{0}+K_{2})}$ which implies that down-hexagons have the lowest energy if $K_{2}<K_{0}$ , whereas roll waves have the lowest energy if $K_{2}\in(K_{0},0)$ . We now establish the energy hierarchy for $\beta_{2}>0$ , see Figure 11 . We first check that for $K_{2}<K_{0}$ , the down-hexagons $\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}}$ are the non-trivial equilibrium with the lowest energy.
