Then, we may compute that $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})\sim\frac{\mu_{0}^{3}}{2\beta_{2}^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})\sim-\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{4K_{0}}\quad\text{as }\mu_{0}\searrow 0.$ In addition, we have $\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})=-\frac{3}{2(K_{0}+2K_{2})}\mu_{0}-\frac{3(-\beta_{2}^{2}+\sqrt{\beta_{2}^{4}-4\beta_{2}^{2}(K_{0}+2K_{2})\mu_{0}})}{4(K_{0}+2K_{2})^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})=-\frac{\mu_{0}}{2K_{0}}.$ We note that $\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})$ is strictly convex and $\tfrac{\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})}{\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})}$ is asymptotic to $\tfrac{3K_{0}}{K_{0}+2K_{2}}$ as $\mu_{0}\to+\infty$ . Hence, if $K_{2}<K_{0}$ , we have $\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})<\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})$ and so the down-hexagon energy never exceeds the roll-wave energy. If $0>K_{2}>K_{0}$ , then $\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})-\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})$ has exactly one root and due to the asymptotic behaviour there is exactly one $\mu_{1}>0$ such that $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})|_{\mu_{0}=\mu_{1}}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})|_{\mu_{0}=\mu_{1}}$ . $\mu_{1}$ can be explicitly calculated and is given by ( 4.16 ). A direct computation shows that for any $K_{2}<0$ , it holds $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{+}},\mathbf{0})-\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})=-\frac{\beta_{2}\left(\beta_{2}^{2}-4\mu_{0}(K_{0}+2K_{2})\right)^{3/2}}{4(K_{0}+2K_{2})^{3}}>0.$ For the mixed modes, we may also compute $\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{M\!M},\mathbf{0})=-\frac{1}{K_{0}+K_{2}}\mu_{0}-\frac{\beta_{2}^{2}}{2(K_{0}-K_{2})(K_{0}+K_{2})},\quad\mu_{0}>-\frac{K_{0}\beta_{2}^{2}}{(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}}.$ If $0>K_{2}>K_{0}$ , it immediately follows that $\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{M\!M},\mathbf{0})>\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})$ and hence $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{M\!M},\mathbf{0})>\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})>\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})$ for $\mu_{0}>-\frac{K_{0}\beta_{2}^{2}}{(K_{0}-K_{2})^{2}}$ . If $K_{2}<K_{0}$ , we find $\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{M\!M},\mathbf{0})-\partial_{\mu_{0}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})\geqslant\frac{K_{0}-K_{2}}{2(K_{0}+2K_{2})(K_{0}+K_{2})}\mu_{0}>0$ and since $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{M\!M},\mathbf{0})|_{\mu=\mu_{M\!M}}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})|_{\mu=\mu_{M\!M}}>\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})|_{\mu=\mu_{M\!M}}$ , we find that $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{M\!M},\mathbf{0})>\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{H_{-}},\mathbf{0})$ . We now again denote the non-trivial equilibrium with the smallest energy by $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ . Since $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})=0$ and $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})>0$ as shown, $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ is in the sublevel set $\{(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\in\mathbb{R}^{6}\,:\,\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})<\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ . Therefore, there is a connected component of the sublevel set which contains $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ . As noted above, we denote this component as $\Omega$ . Using that $\mathcal{H}$ is strictly decreasing along non-stationary orbits, we find that $\Omega$ is forward invariant under the dynamics of ( 4.1 ). Next, we show that $\Omega$ is bounded. To show this, we argue by contradiction and assume that it is unbounded. Then, there exists a path $(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s),\tilde{\mathbf{B}}(s))_{s\geqslant 0}$ with $\|(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s),\tilde{\mathbf{B}}(s))\|\to\infty$ as $s\to\infty$ such that $\mathcal{H}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s),\tilde{\mathbf{B}}(s))<\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ for all $s\geqslant 0$ and $(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(0),\tilde{\mathbf{B}}(0))=(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ . Since $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\geqslant\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{0})$ , the projected path $(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s),\mathbf{0})_{s\geqslant 0}$ also satisfies $\mathcal{H}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s),\mathbf{0})<\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ and $\|\mathbf{A}(s)\|\to\infty$ as $s\to\infty$ . The latter follows by contradiction. Assume that $\|\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s)\|$ is uniformly bounded for $s\geqslant 0$ , then $\|\tilde{\mathbf{B}}(s)\|\to\infty$ and hence, $\mathcal{H}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s),\tilde{\mathbf{B}}(s))\to+\infty$ as $s\to\infty$ . This contradicts the assumption that $\mathcal{H}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s),\tilde{\mathbf{B}(s)})<\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ . We now show that there exists a critical point $(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{0})\neq\mathbf{0}$ with $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{0})<\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ , which contradicts the assumption that $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ is the non-trivial equilibrium with the lowest energy. To obtain the existence of this critical point, we note that $\mathbf{A}\mapsto\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{0})$ satisfies the assumptions of the Mountain Pass theorem, cf. [ Rab86 , Thm. 2.2] . Specifically, the Palais–Smale condition is satisfied since $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{0})\to-\infty$ as $\|\mathbf{A}\|\to\infty$ due to $K_{0}<0$ and $K_{2}<0$ . Hence, a critical value of $\mathbf{A}\mapsto\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{0})$ is given by the infimum of the maximal values along all paths connecting $\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}$ to $\mathbf{A}=\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(\tilde{s})$ for $\tilde{s}$ sufficiently large with $\mathcal{H}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(\tilde{s}),\mathbf{0})<0$ . This infimum is, in particular, bounded above by $\max_{s\in[0,\tilde{s}]}\mathcal{H}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}(s),\mathbf{0})<\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ , the desired contradiction. Using this, we now show that $\Omega$ is a subset of the stable manifold of $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ . For this, we need to show that the $\omega$ -limit set of every point in $\Omega$ consists of $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ only. We note that $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ is the only equilibrium in $\Omega$ as $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ is the non-trivial equilibrium with the lowest energy by assumption. In addition, $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ is a minimum of $\mathcal{H}$ since $\mu_{0}>0$ . Since the Lyapunov function is strictly decreasing along orbits, there are no periodic orbits and thus, any solution with initial value in $\Omega$ converges to $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ since $\Omega$ is bounded. As outlined above, it now remains to show that the unstable manifold of $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ intersects with $\Omega$ . For this, we argue that $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})\in\partial\Omega$ , which follows from the fact that $s\mapsto\mathcal{H}(s\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ is strictly increasing for $s\in(0,1)$ since $\Omega$ contains no equilibria except the trivial one. This yields that the line $\{(s\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})\,:\,s\in(0,1)\}$ is fully contained in $\Omega$ . It is therefore sufficient to show that the unstable eigenspace of the linearisation $\mathfrak{L}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})$ of ( 4.1 ) about $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell})$ intersects with $\Omega$ . Let $\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{6}$ be an element of the unstable eigenspace. Since the unstable manifold is tangential to the unstable eigenspace and $\mathcal{H}$ is a strictly decreasing Lyapunov function, it holds that $\mathcal{H}((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})+t\mathbf{v})<\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ for all $|t|\ll 1$ . Now, we consider the function $s\mapsto\omega_{\varepsilon,\mathbf{v}}(s):=\mathcal{H}(s((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})+\varepsilon\mathbf{v})).$ We want to show that there exists $\varepsilon>0$ such that $\max_{s\in[0,1]}\omega_{\varepsilon,\mathbf{v}}(s)<\mathcal{H}((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0}))$ holds true either for $\mathbf{v}$ or $-\mathbf{v}$ . This ensures that $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})+\varepsilon\mathbf{v}$ lies in the correct component of the sublevel set, i.e. $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})+\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\in\Omega$ . Let $s^{*}(\varepsilon)\in[0,1]$ be the maximal value, and we may assume that $s^{*}(\varepsilon)<1$ since otherwise there is nothing to prove. Since $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})+\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\to(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ for $\varepsilon\to 0$ , we obtain that $s^{*}(\varepsilon)\to 1$ . Note that $s^{*}$ is smooth in a neighbourhood of $\varepsilon=0$ due to the implicit function theorem. We thus make the $\varepsilon$ -expansion about $\varepsilon=0$ given by $s^{*}(\varepsilon)=1+\varepsilon s_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ for some $s_{1}\leqslant 0$ . Inserting this expansion into $\omega_{\varepsilon,\mathbf{v}}$ and expanding again, we obtain $\omega_{\varepsilon,\mathbf{v}}(s^{*}(\varepsilon))=\mathcal{H}((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0}))+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\bigl(s_{1}^{2}\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+2s_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})D^{2}\mathcal{H}((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0}))\mathbf{v}\bigr)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3}),$ where $\kappa_{1}:=(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})D^{2}\mathcal{H}((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0}))(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})<0$ and $\kappa_{2}=\mathbf{v}D^{2}\mathcal{H}((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0}))\mathbf{v}<0$ . By potentially switching the sign of $\mathbf{v}$ , we can ensure that $s_{1}^{2}\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+2s_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})D^{2}\mathcal{H}((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0}))\mathbf{v}<0$ and then choose $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small to obtain $\omega_{\varepsilon,\mathbf{v}}(s^{*}(\varepsilon))<\mathcal{H}((\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0}))$ . Therefore, $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})+\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\in\Omega$ for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small. Since the unstable manifold is tangential to the unstable direction $\mathbf{v}$ this establishes an intersection of the unstable manifold of $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ with $\Omega$ , which completes the proof.
∎ Remark 4.11. We point out that the proof of Theorem 4.10 in particular shows that $\Omega$ is uniformly bounded in $\mathbf{d}$ . Before considering the persistence of the orbits established in Theorem 4.10 , we briefly discuss the existence of other heteroclinic orbits. A fully rigorous construction of such orbits is highly non-trivial due to the high-dimensional phase space and the lack of symmetries, which prevents a direct further reduction to smaller invariant subspaces, with the exception of the invariant subspace containing roll waves as the only non-trivial pattern. However, we conjecture, for example, the existence of heteroclinic orbits connecting the other non-trivial equilibria to the trivial one, as well as a heteroclinic orbit connecting up-hexagons to roll waves for $\mu_{0}>0$ before the bifurcation of the mixed modes from the roll waves. In the fast-front case treated in [ HJ25 ] , such orbits can be found rigorously using phase plane analysis. Here, the former orbits can be found using a numerical shooting algorithm, see Figure 1 for an invasion of up-hexagons into the trivial state and Figures 12a and 12b for an invasion of mixed modes and false hexagons into the trivial state. The construction of the latter orbit is difficult even numerically, and we leave a detailed numerical analysis for future research. However, by performing systematic shooting in directions of the unstable manifold of the equilibrium corresponding to up-hexagons, we find orbits that connect to the trivial equilibrium but come close to the roll-wave equilibrium. The corresponding pattern interface is a two-stage invasion of up-hexagons into the trivial state with a large plateau of roll waves as an intermediate state, see Figure 12c . As discussed in Section 1.2 , this is a typical mechanism of how hexagons arise in experiments. a Pattern interface connecting mixed modes to the trivial state. b Pattern interface connecting false hexagons to the trivial state. c Two-stage invasion of up-hexagons into the trivial state via roll waves. Figure 12 : Example of a two-dimensional planar pattern interface corresponding to numerically obtained heteroclinic orbits. 4.3  Persistence of heteroclinic orbits We now discuss the persistence of heteroclinic orbits in the leading-order system ( 4.1 ) as solutions to the full reduced equation on the centre manifold ( 3.32 ). For this, we first note that the equilibria obtained in Corollary 4.1 are hyperbolic outside of bifurcation points and therefore, as discussed in [ HJ25 ] , the bifurcation diagram persists. Next, for $\mu_{0}>0$ , we consider heteroclinic orbits in the leading-order system ( 4.1 ), which connect a non-trivial equilibrium $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ to the trivial equilibrium $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ . For this, we note the heteroclinic orbit lies in both the stable manifold of $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ and the unstable manifold of $(\mathbf{A}_{\ell},\mathbf{0})$ and thus, they intersect. Since the stable manifold of the trivial equilibrium $(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0})$ is a six-dimensional manifold in $\mathbb{R}^{6}$ , this intersection is transversal and persists under perturbation. This yields the following result. Theorem 4.12. Let $\mu_{0}$ and $\theta\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{6})$ with $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ . Any heteroclinic orbit of the leading-order system ( 4.1 ), which connects to the trivial equilibrium, persists as a solution to the full reduced equation ( 3.32 ). In particular, the heteroclinic orbits constructed in Theorem 4.10 persist. We point out that the persistence of other heteroclinic orbits, which do not connect to the trivial state, is less obvious. For example, we consider a potential connection between up-hexagons and roll waves for $\mu_{0}>0$ before the bifurcation of mixed modes from the roll waves. Recalling the dimensions of the respective stable and unstable manifolds depicted in Figure 9 , we find that, in this regime, the unstable manifold of the up-hexagons is three-dimensional and the stable manifold of the roll waves is four-dimensional (since the equilibria are hyperbolic). Therefore, we must show that the intersection of a three-dimensional manifold with a four-dimensional manifold persists in a six-dimensional phase space. Counting dimensions, the intersections between these manifolds will generically be transversal and thus persistent. However, to the best of our knowledge, there is no general result that excludes the occurrence of degenerate situations in which the intersection is not transversal. Therefore, the persistence of such orbits, even if they can be constructed in the leading-order system, remains an open question. 4.4  The singular limits $\theta\to\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ and $c_{0}\to\infty$ We now study the behaviour of the reduced system ( 3.32 ) when $\theta\to\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . For this, we define the small parameter $\delta:=4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}$ . Then, we can write the reduced equations ( 3.32 ) for $\theta<\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ as $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}&=B_{1},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{1}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}A_{1}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}B_{1}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}&=B_{2},\\
\delta\partial_{\Xi}B_{2}&=-\mu_{0}A_{2}-c_{0}B_{2}-\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}-(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}&=B_{3},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{3}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}A_{3}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}B_{3}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\end{split}$ (4.17) which can be written in the abstract form $\begin{split}\delta\partial_{\Xi}B_{2}&=F(B_{2},Y;\delta),\\
\partial_{\Xi}Y&=G(B_{2},Y;\delta),\end{split}$ (4.18) with $Y=(A_{1},B_{1},A_{2},A_{3},B_{3})$ , which is a fast-slow system, see e.g. [ Kue15 ] . We then define the critical manifold $C_{0}$ , given by $C_{0}:=\{(B_{2},Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{6}\,:\,F(B_{2},Y;0)=0\}$ which is obtained by setting $\delta=0$ in ( 4.18 ). The flow on $C_{0}$ is obtained by solving $F(B_{2},Y;0)$ for $B_{2}$ and substituting this result into $\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}=B_{2}$ . The resulting system then reads as $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}&=B_{1},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{1}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}A_{1}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}B_{1}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}&=-\mu_{0}A_{2}-\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}-(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}&=B_{3},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{3}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}A_{3}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}B_{3}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{3})^{2}}(\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\end{split}$ (4.19) which is equivalent to the reduced equation ( 3.37 ) for $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Let $(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{het},\delta},\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{het},\delta})$ a uniformly bounded sequence of heteroclinic orbits with $\delta$ such that the corresponding angle $\theta$ satisfies $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ . Then, our goal is to show that this sequence converges to a heteroclinic orbit $(\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{het},0},\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{het},0})$ solving ( 3.37 ). Fix any such sequence and let $\mathcal{S}_{0}$ be a compact submanifold of $C_{0}$ which is sufficiently large to contain $Y_{\delta}$ for $\delta>0$ . We now check that $\mathcal{S}_{0}$ is normally hyperbolic since $D_{B_{2}}F(B_{2},Y;0)=-c_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\neq 0$ for fixed $c_{0}>0$ and $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small. Therefore, standard Fenichel theory applies, see e.g. [ Kue15 , Thm. 3.1.4] , and we obtain a sequence of slow manifolds $\mathcal{S}_{\delta}$ with the following properties. For every $\delta>0$ , $\mathcal{S}_{\delta}$ is a locally invariant manifold of ( 4.17 ), and it is diffeomorphic to $\mathcal{S}_{0}$ . Moreover, the flow on $\mathcal{S}_{\delta}$ converges to the slow flow generated by ( 4.19 ). Finally, we note that any solution $\Xi\mapsto(\mathbf{A}_{\delta}(\Xi),\mathbf{B}_{\delta}(\Xi))$ to ( 4.17 ) that is uniformly bounded in $\Xi\in\mathbb{R}$ is also contained in $\mathcal{S}_{\delta}$ , after potentially choosing a larger compact submanifold of $C_{0}$ . For this, we first note that slow manifolds are centre manifolds of the fast system, i.e. ( 4.17 ) after a change of variables to the fast time $\tilde{\Xi}=\delta^{-1}\Xi$ , extended with $\partial_{\tilde{\Xi}}\delta=0$ . Then, we can lift the typical observation that solutions that are globally contained in a small neighbourhood lie on the centre manifold, see e.g. [ HI11 ] to slow manifolds by using a finite covering exploiting that $S_{0}$ is a compact submanifold. Note that, although centre manifolds (or slow manifolds) are not unique, bounded solutions are contained in all centre manifolds, see e.g. [ Sij85 ] , and therefore, we write ‘the’ centre manifold. In particular, this shows that equilibria and heteroclinic orbits of ( 4.17 ) are contained in $\mathcal{S}_{\delta}$ . Since the heteroclinic orbits from Theorem 4.10 are uniformly bounded in $\theta$ and persistent by Theorem 4.12 , we obtain the following result for $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Theorem 4.13. Let $c_{0}>0$ . There exist heteroclinic orbits in ( 4.19 ), or equivalently in ( 3.37 ), with the same properties as in Theorem 4.10 . To conclude this subsection, we discuss the case of solutions for $c_{0}$ large. For this, we treat $\tfrac{1}{c_{0}^{2}}$ as a small parameter. Then, the corresponding slow subsystem for $c_{0}\to\infty$ reads as $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}+\mu_{0}A_{1}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{2}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{2}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}+\mu_{0}A_{2}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{3}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{3}|^{2}))A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{3}+\mu_{0}A_{3}+\beta_{2}\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{3}|^{2}+K_{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2}))A_{3}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0.\end{split}$ (4.20) We highlight that the system ( 4.20 ) is independent of the direction $\mathbf{d}$ , which was already pointed out in [ HJ25 , Rem. 5.1(1)] , where the system ( 4.20 ) was obtained as a reduced equation on a centre manifold in the construction of fast-moving pattern interfaces in an asymptotic model for the three-dimensional Bénard–Marangoni problem. A detailed analysis of heteroclinic orbits occurring in the slow subsystem can be found in [ HJ25 , Sec. 5.5] by exploiting that the system ( 4.20 ) has a two-dimensional invariant subspace $\{A_{2}=A_{3}\in\mathbb{R}\}$ , which contains all equilibria. As shown in [ Doe+03 ] , these orbits persist in the full system ( 3.32 ) with $c_{0}$ large, using similar arguments as above. 4.5  Pattern interfaces corresponding to heteroclinic orbits Before analysing the dynamics of the reduced equations numerically, we analyse the solutions in the full pattern-forming system ( 1.1 ) that correspond to heteroclinic orbits in the reduced equations ( 3.32 ) and ( 3.37 ). For this, we find from the ansatz ( 3.21 ) in the derivation of the reduced equations and using the centre manifold theorem 3.10 that the spatial-dynamics system ( 1.11 ) has a solution $W=W(\xi,p)$ with first component given by $W_{0}(\xi,\mathbf{p})=\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{3}A_{j}(\varepsilon\xi)e^{i\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p}}+c.c.+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})$ where we used that the eigenvectors $\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}_{\pm}$ are normalised such that their first component is equal to one and that $A_{j}=\tilde{A}_{j,+}+\tilde{A}_{j,-}$ . Furthermore, we recall that $W(\xi,\mathbf{p})=(U,(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})U(\xi,\mathbf{p}),(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})^{2}U(\xi,\mathbf{p}),(\partial_{\xi}+\mathbf{d}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{p}})^{3}U(\xi,\mathbf{p}))$ and that $u(t,\mathbf{x})=U(\xi,\mathbf{p})$ is a solution to ( 1.1 ). We therefore obtain the following result. Theorem 4.14. Fix $\mathbf{d}=(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))\in S^{1}$ such that $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ and let $c_{0}>0$ . Then, for any bounded orbit $\Xi\mapsto(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})(\Xi)$ of ( 3.32 ) there exists an $\varepsilon_{0}>0$ such that for all $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ the pattern-forming equation ( 1.1 ) has solutions of the form $u(t,\mathbf{x})=2\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{3}A_{j}(\varepsilon(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\varepsilon c_{0}t))\cos(\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).$ Specifically, there exist open classes of suitable polynomial nonlinearities such that ( 1.1 ) has solutions $u(t,\mathbf{x})=U(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}+\varepsilon c_{0}t,\mathbf{x})$ with $\lim_{\xi\to-\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=-2\varepsilon A_{\mathrm{hex}}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\cos(\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\quad\text{and}\lim_{\xi\to\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=0$ for some $A_{\mathrm{hex}}>0$ , cf. ( 4.4 ), or $\lim_{\xi\to-\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=2\varepsilon A_{\mathrm{roll}}\cos(x_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\quad\text{and}\lim_{\xi\to\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=0$ for some $A_{\mathrm{roll}}>0$ , cf. ( 4.3 ). 5  Pattern interfaces on a square lattice So far, the construction was restricted to patterns which lie on a hexagonal Fourier lattice generated by $\mathbf{k}_{1}$ , $\mathbf{k}_{2}$ and $\mathbf{k}_{3}$ given in ( 1.3 ). Notably, this lattice does not support square patterns, which are generated by the Fourier wave vectors $\mathbf{k}_{1}=(1,0)$ and $\mathbf{k}_{2}=(0,1)$ . Therefore, we now discuss how to obtain pattern interfaces on this square Fourier lattice $\Gamma_{\square}$ . Since the construction largely follows that of the hexagonal lattice and is significantly easier because the square lattice is non-resonant, we only report the differences. In particular, the full analysis can be carried out without using Assumption B . 5.1  Spectral analysis Consider now a direction $\mathbf{d}=(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))\in S^{1}$ with $\theta\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{4})$ . We reiterate that it is sufficient to consider only $0\leqslant\theta<\tfrac{\pi}{4}$ since all other angles can be obtained by using the $D_{4}$ -symmetry of ( 1.1 ). We note that the ansatz ( 1.9 ) and the resulting spatial dynamics formulation ( 1.11 ) do not depend on the specific choice of Fourier lattice and therefore, do not change. Similarly, the geometric interpretation of the eigenvalue problem given by Lemmas 2.1 and 2.2 is unchanged, see Figure 13 . A direct consequence of the geometric interpretation of the spectrum is that a more-central Jordan block of size four now occurs for $\theta=0$ instead of $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ since $\mathbf{k}_{2}\perp(1,0)$ . Therefore, Proposition 2.3 holds verbatim with the condition $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ replaced by $\theta=0$ . Next, we note that the angle condition for the presence of a spectral gap given in Proposition 2.4 changes from $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ to $\cot(\theta)\in\mathbb{Q}$ , which follows from the same algebraic computations using that $\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}=-n_{1}\sin(\theta)+n_{2}\cos(\theta)$ for $\boldsymbol{\gamma}=n_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1}+n_{2}\mathbf{k}_{2}\in\Gamma_{\square}$ . To conclude the spectral analysis, we find that there are eight more-central eigenvalues (for $\theta\neq 0$ ) or six (for $\theta=0$ ) which still have the expansions ( 2.6 ) and ( 2.7 ), respectively. The remaining statements of Lemmas 2.5 and 2.6 and Proposition 2.7 hold unchanged under the angle condition $\cot(\theta)\in\mathbb{Q}$ . a b Figure 13 : Geometric characterisation of the spectrum of $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}$ on a square lattice ( 13a ) for an angle $\theta$ satisfying $\cot(\theta)\in\mathbb{Q}$ . Therefore, the hyperbolic lattice points have positive distance to the blue shaded strip and $\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{0}$ has a spectral gap around the imaginary axis ( 13b ) . 5.2  Centre manifold theory and reduced equations In the centre manifold theory, we first remark that the functional-analytic setup is independent of a specific choice of Fourier lattice, and thus holds unchanged. The projections can then be defined in the same way, and the estimates in Lemma 3.3 hold with the natural changes to the angle. We then define the splitting of $W$ into more-central, less-central, and hyperbolic parts as in ( 3.6 ) and introduce the same rescaled variables ( 3.7 ), as well as the resulting system ( 3.8 ). The main change compared to the hexagonal case is in the following normal-form analysis. First, since the square lattice is non-resonant, we find $\mathcal{P}_{mc}^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{k}_{j})\hat{\mathcal{N}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{mc};\mathbf{k}_{j},\vartheta)=0$ . In particular, this implies that Assumption B is not necessary in the square case. Second, we find that in $\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{\mathcal{N}}}_{lc}(\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{mc},\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{lc}+\underaccent{\vphantom{W}\smash{\raisebox{1.59735pt}{\kern 0.0pt\rule{5.58054pt}{0.6pt}}}}{\hat{W}}_{h};\boldsymbol{\gamma},\vartheta)$ the same problematic nonlinearities appear. Therefore, we make the near-identity transformation, which is still given by ( 3.9 ) and results in the equations ( 3.10 ) for $\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{3}$ . While we obtain a $\mathcal{O}(1)$ -bound for $\mathcal{B}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{B}_{3}$ already in the hexagonal case, $\mathcal{B}_{2}$ did not have a uniform bound due to the resonance of the hexagonal lattice, cf. Lemma 3.5 and Figure 5 . This simplifies in the square case since there are no two lattice points in $\Gamma_{\square}$ that generate less-central eigenvalues with the same imaginary part and have distance one. Therefore, we find that $\mathcal{B}_{2}$ is also uniformly bounded as $\varepsilon\to 0$ . With this normal-form transformation, Lemma 3.7 holds unchanged, and in particular, the nonlinearities in the transformed equations satisfy the Lipschitz bounds ( 3.13 ) with the same scaling in $\varepsilon$ . Additionally, the semigroup bounds hold unchanged after replacing the angle condition with $\cot(\theta)\in\mathbb{Q}$ and distinguishing the case $\theta=0$ . Therefore, we obtain a centre manifold theorem as in Theorem 3.10 for $\theta\neq 0$ and Theorem 3.15 for $\theta=0$ . However, the resulting centre manifolds are of size $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2/3+\delta})$ for any chosen $\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{3})$ as in the one-dimensional case [ HS99 ] . Next, we consider the reduced equations on the centre manifold. Recalling that there are eight, respectively six, more-central eigenvalues, and repeating the same derivation as in Section 3.4 , we obtain the reduced equations in the case $\theta\neq 0$ as $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}&=B_{1},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{1}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}A_{1}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}B_{1}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}}(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{1}|A_{2}|^{2})A_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}&=B_{2},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{2}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}A_{2}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}B_{2}-\dfrac{1}{4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}}(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{1}|A_{1}|^{2})A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\end{split}$ (5.1) where $K_{0}$ is given by ( 3.31 ) and $K_{1}$ is given by $\begin{split}K_{1}&:=2\left[\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i\mathbf{0}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1})\nu_{0}+\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i(\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{-i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1})\nu_{\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}}+\hat{N}_{2}(e^{i(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1})\nu_{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{k}_{2}}\right]\\
&\quad+6\hat{N}_{3}(e^{i\mathbf{k}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}},e^{-i\mathbf{k}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}};\mathbf{k}_{1}).\end{split}$ Note that ( 5.1 ) can be written as the second-order system $\begin{split}4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{1}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}+\mu_{0}A_{1}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{1}|A_{2}|^{2})A_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
4(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}\partial_{\Xi}^{2}A_{2}+c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}+\mu_{0}A_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{1}|A_{1}|^{2})A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)&=0,\\
\end{split}$ (5.2) In the case $\theta=0$ , this reduces to the three-dimensional first-order system $\begin{split}\partial_{\Xi}A_{1}&=B_{1},\\
\partial_{\Xi}B_{1}&=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{4}A_{1}-\dfrac{c_{0}}{4}B_{1}-\dfrac{1}{4}(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{1}|A_{2}|^{2})A_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\\
c_{0}\partial_{\Xi}A_{2}&=-\mu_{0}A_{2}-(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{1}|A_{1}|^{2})A_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),\end{split}$ (5.3) which can again be recovered from the system ( 5.1 ) in the limit $\theta\to 0$ via a fast-slow analysis as in Section 4.4 . In particular, we note that there is no quadratic term since the square lattice is non-resonant. 5.3  Analysis of reduced equations and pattern interfaces We conclude this section by discussing the dynamics of the reduced equations in the square case, specifically, the existence and stability of equilibria and heteroclinic connections between them. As in the hexagonal case, we restrict to the case $\theta\neq 0$ since the results for $\theta=0$ can be recovered via a fast-slow analysis. Additionally, we first analyse the leading order equation obtained from ( 5.1 ) for $\varepsilon=0$ . A direct computation shows that the following equilibria exist, see Figure 14 for typical patterns. Corollary 5.1. The system ( 5.1 ) at $\varepsilon=0$ has the following equilibrium solutions: (a) The trivial solution $\mathbf{A}_{T}:=(A_{1,T},A_{2,T})=(0,0).$ (b) Roll waves $\mathbf{A}_{R}=(A_{1,R},A_{2,R})=\Big(\pm\sqrt{-\frac{\mu_{0}}{K_{0}}},0\Big)$ (5.4) if $\mu_{0}K_{0}<0$ . (c) Squares $\mathbf{A}_{S}=(A_{1,S},A_{2,S})\quad\text{with}\quad|A_{j,S}|=\sqrt{-\frac{\mu_{0}}{K_{0}+K_{1}}}$ (5.5) if $\mu_{0}(K_{0}+K_{1})<0$ . a Roll waves b Squares Figure 14 : Stationary pattern on a square lattice obtained in Corollary 5.1 . Their stability properties in the spatial-dynamics system ( 5.1 ) can again be characterised by the linear stability in the Landau equations $\begin{split}\partial_{T}A_{1}&=\mu_{0}A_{1}+(K_{0}|A_{1}|^{2}+K_{1}|A_{2}|^{2})A_{1},\\
\partial_{T}A_{2}&=\mu_{0}A_{2}+(K_{0}|A_{2}|^{2}+K_{1}|A_{1}|^{2})A_{2},\end{split}$ see also Remark 4.6 . Restricting to the case $K_{0}<0$ and $K_{1}<0$ , we find the bifurcation diagram of Figure 15 . a $K_{2}<K_{0}<0$ b $K_{0}<K_{2}<0$ Figure 15 : Bifurcation diagram of stationary patterns on the square lattice for different parameter regimes. For the heteroclinic orbits, we first point out that also the leading-order system given by ( 5.1 ) with $\varepsilon=0$ has a Lyapunov function given by $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2}|B_{1}|^{2}+(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{2})^{2}|B_{2}|^{2}+\dfrac{\mu_{0}}{2}(|A_{1}|^{2}+|A_{2}|^{2})+\dfrac{K_{0}}{4}(|A_{1}|^{4}+|A_{2}|^{4})+\dfrac{K_{1}}{2}A_{1}^{2}A_{2}^{2},$ which is strictly decreasing along orbits since it satisfies ( 4.15 ) along a solution. Calculating the energies at the non-trivial equilibria, we find that $\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{4K_{0}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}_{S},\mathbf{0})=-\dfrac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{2(K_{0}+K_{1})}.$ For $\mu_{0}>0$ , we therefore can follow the same strategy as in Theorem 4.10 to obtain heteroclinic orbits between roll waves and the trivial state for $K_{0}<K_{1}<0$ and between squares and the trivial state for $K_{1}<K_{0}$ . These orbits persist for $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small again using the fact that, for $\mu_{0}>0$ , the trivial equilibrium is stable and thus the orbits lie in an intersection of the unstable manifold of either roll waves or squares with the four-dimensional stable manifold of the trivial equilibrium. Since the phase space is $\mathbb{R}^{4}$ , these intersections are transversal. Summarising the above discussion, including the fast-slow structure for $\theta\to 0$ , we thus obtain the following result. Theorem 5.2. Let $\theta\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{4})$ , $c_{0}>0$ and $K_{0}<0$ . (a) If $K_{1}<K_{0}$ , for every $\mu_{0}>0$ there exist heteroclinic orbits $(\mathbf{A}_{S\to T},\mathbf{B}_{S\to T})$ in ( 5.1 ) and in ( 5.3 ) such that $\lim_{\Xi\to-\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{S\to T},\mathbf{B}_{S\to T})=(\mathbf{A}_{S},\mathbf{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\Xi\to+\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{S\to T},\mathbf{B}_{S\to T})=(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0}).$ (b) If $K_{0}<K_{1}<0$ , for every $\mu_{0}>0$ there exist heteroclinic orbits $(\mathbf{A}_{R\to T},\mathbf{B}_{R\to T})$ in ( 5.1 ) and in ( 5.3 ) such that $\lim_{\Xi\to-\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{R\to T},\mathbf{B}_{R\to T})=(\mathbf{A}_{R},\mathbf{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\Xi\to+\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{R\to T},\mathbf{B}_{R\to T})=(\mathbf{A}_{T},\mathbf{0}).$ Following the discussion in Section 4.5 we obtain the existence of pattern interfaces on a square lattice in ( 1.1 ) from heteroclinic orbits in ( 5.1 ), cf. Theorem 4.14 . Specifically, there exist $\varepsilon_{0}>0$ and open classes of suitable polynomial nonlinearities such that for any $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})$ , equation ( 1.1 ) has solutions $u(t,\mathbf{x})=U(\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}+\varepsilon c_{0}t,\mathbf{x})$ with $\lim_{\xi\to-\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=2\varepsilon A_{\mathrm{sq}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\cos(\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{p})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\quad\text{and}\lim_{\xi\to\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=0$ for some $A_{\mathrm{sq}}>0$ , cf. ( 5.5 ), or $\lim_{\xi\to-\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=2\varepsilon A_{\mathrm{roll}}\cos(x_{1})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\quad\text{and}\lim_{\xi\to\infty}U(\xi,\mathbf{p})=0$ for some $A_{\mathrm{roll}}>0$ , cf. ( 5.4 ). a Pattern interface for angle $\theta=0$ . b Pattern interface for angle $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{4}$ . Figure 16 : Panels ( 16a ) and ( 16b ) show pattern interfaces describing the invasion of square patterns into the unstable homogeneous state for different angles. 6  Discussion In this paper, we construct slow-moving pattern interfaces with general spreading direction in a two-dimensional Swift–Hohenberg-type equation. These interfaces describe the invasion of a linearly unstable, spatially homogeneous state by either roll waves or planar hexagonal patterns. We also present evidence for the existence of two-stage invasion processes, where the homogeneous state is first invaded by roll waves, which in turn are invaded by hexagonal patterns through a secondary front interface. We conclude this discussion with a brief overview of related questions and potential pathways for further investigation. Front selection While it is well-understood that planar patterns, specifically hexagonal patterns, arise through planar or radial pattern interfaces, the exact mechanism is not understood rigorously. In the spatially one-dimensional case, some rigorous selection results are available. In particular, for fronts in reaction-diffusion equations such as the Fisher–KPP equation, a rigorous theory for the selected speed has been developed recently, and we refer to [ AS22 ] and to the recent overview [ AHS25 ] . Moreover, rigorous selection results for pattern-forming fronts specifically have only been obtained very recently [ ACR26 ] . However, in the two-dimensional case considered here, many open problems remain regarding (a) the selected invasion speed and (b) the selected pattern in the wake when starting from a compactly supported perturbation of the linearly unstable trivial state. The two-dimensional situation is even more complicated since, in experiments, the selection of planar patterns is facilitated through a cascade of invading fronts, which typically do not travel with the same speed. An example for this is that the hexagonal pattern in thermal convection typically forms as a secondary invasion to roll waves, which are the selected pattern in the leading edge of the front [ PN94 , CM99 ] . The main challenge in understanding these structures rigorously is the fact that the connecting state is unstable and that the amplitude solution is not a travelling wave. This is an open question even for one-dimensional reaction-diffusion systems, and we refer to [ GH25 ] for first linear stability results. Rigorous heteroclinics While we construct selected heteroclinic orbits analytically in Section 4.2 , these only cover parts of the orbits that we find numerically, cf. Figures 1 , 2 and 12 . Specifically, the rigorous existence of two-stage invasion processes corresponding to heteroclinic orbits from up-hexagons to the trivial state that pass close to the roll waves remains open. The main problem is that the phase space of the reduced equations is still six-dimensional, which makes any global analysis challenging even though the reduced equations have a strictly decreasing Lyapunov function. The partial results of this paper rely on the observation that the energy landscape is topologically very simple close to the trivial equilibrium allowing for the construction of a sufficiently large trapping region that has an intersection with the unstable manifold of the non-trivial equilibrium with the lowest energy. Beyond that trapping region, the energy landscape is topologically more complicated and more involved methods have to be used. One such topological method to construct heteroclinic orbits is Conley index theory [ Con78 , MM02 ] , which has already been mentioned in [ Doe+03 ] as a potential pathway. Extending the set of allowed angles The construction of planar interfaces in the paper is restricted to directions satisfying the condition $\cot(\theta)\in\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ , which guarantees the existence of a spectral gap around the imaginary axis at $\varepsilon=0$ , see Proposition 2.4 . The case $\cot(\theta)\notin\sqrt{3}\mathbb{Q}$ is much more challenging since there exists a sequence of hyperbolic eigenvalues which accumulate at the imaginary axis at infinity. This suggests the presence of a small-divisor problem, which could potentially be solved using Kolmogorov–Arnold–Moser (KAM) theory to obtain pattern interfaces in directions satisfying a Diophantine condition. However, we expect that this is a technical challenge and that the reduced dynamics to leading order are still given by ( 1.6 ) obtained from the amplitude equations. Nevertheless, the proof of this remains an open problem. Data availability statement The explicit computations for the front speed via a marginal stability analysis were performed using Mathematica [ Mat ] . The numerical simulations of orbits in the leading-order system ( 4.1 ) were done using Julia [ Bez+17 ] . This was also used to plot the pattern interfaces using the leading-order expressions in Theorem 4.14 with the numerically obtained amplitudes. Finally, the simulations of the selected front speeds presented in Appendix B were done using FEniCSx [ Bar+23 ] . The code used to generate the corresponding data is available under [ HJ26 ] . Appendix A Spectral analysis in generic rotation-symmetric pattern-forming systems close to a Turing instability Although we have hinted the genericity of the spectral results obtained in Propositions 2.3 , 2.4 and 2.7 in the above analysis, we now discuss this in more detail. The main observation is that the spectral properties are generic for systems which are (i) rotation-symmetric and (ii) close to a Turing instability. More precisely, we consider a system of the form $\partial_{t}u=\Lambda_{\mu}(\nabla)u+N(u)$ , where $\Lambda_{\mu}(\nabla)$ is a linear operator with polynomial symbol depending on a parameter $\mu$ and $N$ is a generic, smooth nonlinearity.
We assume that the system is rotation-symmetric and in particular, the corresponding Fourier symbol $\hat{\Lambda}_{\mu}(\mathbf{k})=e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\Lambda_{\mu}(\nabla)e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}$ satisfies $\hat{\Lambda}_{\mu}(\mathbf{k})=\hat{\Lambda}_{\mu}(|\mathbf{k}|)$ for all $\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ . At every wave vector $\mathbf{k}$ , the Fourier symbol $\hat{\Lambda}_{\mu}(\mathbf{k})$ we denote the eigenvalue with the largest real part by $\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{k};\mu)=\lambda_{\max}(|\mathbf{k}|;\mu)$ . We further assume that the system is close to a Turing instability, which means that there is a critical wave number $\tilde{k}>0$ and a critical parameter $\tilde{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}$ such that (a) all eigenvalues of $\hat{\Lambda}_{\tilde{\mu}}(\mathbf{k})$ have negative real parts for all $\mathbf{k}$ with $|\mathbf{k}|\neq\tilde{k}$ ; (b) $\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{k};\tilde{\mu})=0$ if and only if $|\mathbf{k}|=\tilde{k}$ and zero is a simple eigenvalue; (c) $\partial_{\mu}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{k};\tilde{\mu})=\kappa>0$ for all $|\mathbf{k}|=\tilde{k}$ ; (d) $\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{k};\tilde{\mu})=-\alpha(|\mathbf{k}|-\tilde{k})^{2}+\mathcal{O}((|\mathbf{k}|-\tilde{k})^{3})$ for $\alpha=-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{|\mathbf{k}|}^{2}\lambda_{\max}(|\mathbf{k}|;\tilde{\mu})|_{|\mathbf{k}|=\tilde{k}}>0$ and all $\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ in a neighbourhood of $|\mathbf{k}|=\tilde{k}$ . We refer to Figure 17 for the typical behaviour of the principal eigenvalue curve $\lambda_{\max}$ . Without loss of generality, we can assume that $\tilde{k}=1$ and $\tilde{\mu}=0$ . In addition, we set the scaling $\mu=\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}$ and $c=\varepsilon c_{0}$ as above. a $\mu<\tilde{\mu}$ b $\mu=\tilde{\mu}$ c $\mu>\tilde{\mu}$ Figure 17 : Schematic depiction of the eigenvalue curve with the largest real part close to the Turing instability as $\mu$ transitions through $\tilde{\mu}$ . As in Lemma 2.1 , we find that the eigenvalues $\nu$ of the corresponding spatial-dynamics formulation are given by the roots of the $p(\nu;\boldsymbol{\gamma},\varepsilon)=\det(\Lambda_{\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}}(i\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\nu)+\varepsilon c_{0}\nu I)=\det(\hat{\Lambda}_{\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-i\mathbf{d}\nu)+\varepsilon c_{0}\nu I),$ cf. [ HJ25 , Lem. 5.3] . Due to the simplicity of the zero eigenvalue $\lambda_{\max}$ at $\varepsilon=0$ and $|\mathbf{k}|=1$ , we can write $p(\nu;\boldsymbol{\gamma},\varepsilon)=f(\nu,\varepsilon)(-\alpha(|\boldsymbol{\gamma}-i\mathbf{d}\nu|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-1)^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\kappa\mu_{0}+\varepsilon c_{0}\nu)+\mathcal{O}((|i\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\nu|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-1)^{3}+\varepsilon^{3})),$ where $|\omega|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=\sqrt{\omega^{T}\omega}$ for $\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{2}$ and $f(\nu,\varepsilon)$ is given by the product of the non-critical eigenvalues of $\hat{\Lambda}_{\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-i\mathbf{d}\nu)+\varepsilon c_{0}\nu I$ . This product is non-zero if $\nu$ is contained in a sufficiently small strip around the imaginary axis, since the non-critical eigenvalues of $\hat{\Lambda}_{0}(\mathbf{k})$ have strictly negative real parts for all $\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ . For $\varepsilon=0$ , it is straightforward to check that the geometric characterisation of the spectrum depicted in Figure 4 holds true. Therefore, the spectral properties obtained in Propositions 2.3 and 2.4 are generic for rotation-symmetric pattern-forming systems close to a Turing instability. To obtain the spectral properties for $\varepsilon>0$ we consider how the eigenvalues on the imaginary axis at $\varepsilon=0$ perturb for $\varepsilon>0$ . For this, we assume that the spatial eigenvalue $\nu(\varepsilon)=i\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)$ with $\nu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}$ . From the geometric interpretation for $\varepsilon=0$ we know that $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:=\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\nu_{0}$ lies on the unit circle, see Figure 5 . We first consider the case of lattice points $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ such that $\ell_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}s\,:\,s\in\mathbb{R}\}$ intersects transversally with the unit circle, that is, $\mathbf{d}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\neq 0$ . Using the expansion $|\boldsymbol{\gamma}-i\mathbf{d}(i\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}(\varepsilon))|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=|\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\nu_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-i(\mathbf{d}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)+\mathcal{O}(|\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)|^{2}),$ and using again that $|\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\nu_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=1$ , we find that the correction $\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)$ is given by a solution to the equation $\alpha(\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{2}\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)^{2}+\varepsilon c_{0}(i\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}(\varepsilon))+\varepsilon^{2}\kappa\mu_{0}+\mathcal{O}(|\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)|^{3}+\varepsilon^{3})=0.$ Depending on $\nu_{0}=0$ or $\nu_{0}\neq 0$ , we thus find two different mechanisms for the perturbation of the eigenvalues. If $\nu_{0}=0$ , the natural scaling for $\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)$ is $\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon\delta+h.o.t.$ and $\delta\in\mathbb{C}$ is given by the roots of the quadratic equation $\alpha(\mathbf{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{2}\delta^{2}+c_{0}\delta+\kappa\mu_{0}=0$ since $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ for $\nu_{0}=0$ . Noting that $\alpha>0$ and $\kappa\mu_{0}>0$ since we are in the Turing unstable regime, and $c_{0}>0$ by assumption, we thus find that the roots $\delta$ have negative real part, which implies that the eigenvalues perturb into the left half-plane for $\varepsilon>0$ . In fact, this is fully in line with the results for the stability of equilibria in the reduced equation on the centre manifold given in Lemma 4.4 , where PDE instability implies that the linearisation of the spatial dynamics system only has stable eigenvalues. If $\nu_{0}\neq 0$ , the natural scaling for $\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)$ is $\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon^{1/2}\delta+h.o.t.$ and $\delta\in\mathbb{C}$ is given by the roots of the quadratic equation $\alpha(\mathbf{d}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})^{2}\delta^{2}+c_{0}i\nu_{0}=0.$ Since $\alpha,c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}$ , we find that $\delta^{2}$ is purely imaginary and thus a spatial eigenvalue with non-zero imaginary part at $\varepsilon=0$ splits into an eigenvalue with negative real part and an eigenvalue with positive real part for $\varepsilon>0$ . Notably, the leading order correction of a less-central eigenvalue is therefore independent of the distance of the instability encoded by $\mu_{0}$ , and the splitting only relies on the advection term generated by the front speed $\varepsilon c_{0}$ . In the degenerate case $\mathbf{d}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=0$ , we find that $|\boldsymbol{\gamma}-i\mathbf{d}(i\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}(\varepsilon))|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=|\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\mathbf{d}\nu_{0}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}+\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)^{2}+\mathcal{O}(|\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)|^{3}).$ Therefore, the correction $\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)$ is given by a solution to the equation $-\alpha\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)^{4}+\varepsilon c_{0}(i\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}(\varepsilon))+\varepsilon^{2}\kappa\mu_{0}+\mathcal{O}(|\nu_{1}(\varepsilon)|^{5}+\varepsilon^{3})=0.$ If $\nu_{0}=0$ , this equation has one solution $\nu_{1}=-\varepsilon\tfrac{\kappa\mu_{0}}{c_{0}}$ and three solutions with $\nu_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1/3})$ . If $\nu_{0}\neq 0$ , there are four solutions with $\nu_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1/4})$ . In both cases, the persistence of these solutions follows as in the proof of Proposition 2.7 by assuming that $|\varepsilon\nu_{1}|$ is sufficiently small. We therefore fully recover the spectral properties obtained in Proposition 2.7 for generic rotation-symmetric pattern-forming systems close to a Turing instability. Specifically, in the Swift–Hohenberg equation, we have $\alpha=4$ and $\kappa=1$ , and therefore, the general calculations recover the expansions ( 2.6 ) and ( 2.7 ). Appendix B Numerical front speed selection We conclude the paper by showing the results of some numerical experiments to estimate the selected front speed in the full dynamical problem for a Swift–Hohenberg-type equation ( 1.1 ). Therefore, we simulate the quadratic-cubic Swift–Hohenberg equation $\partial_{t}u=-(1+\Delta)^{2}u+\mu u-\beta|\nabla u|^{2}-u^{3},$ (B.1) proposed in [ Doe+03 ] , with $\mu=\varepsilon^{2}\mu_{0}$ , $\beta=\varepsilon\beta_{2}$ , $\mu_{0}=\beta_{2}=1$ , and $\varepsilon=0.3$ on the rectangle $\Omega=[-4\pi,36\pi]\times[-3\sqrt{3}\pi,3\sqrt{3}\pi]$ subject to homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions using a finite-element method. The equation ( B.1 ) is recast as the second-order system $\begin{split}\partial_{t}u&=\mu u-\beta|\nabla u|^{2}-u^{3}-w-\Delta w,\\
w&=u+\Delta u\end{split}$ in a mixed finite-element formulation, and then discretised with continuous piecewise-linear finite elements on an unstructured triangular mesh with $1884\times 326$ mesh points, which is a spatial resolution of $\Delta x=\tfrac{1}{15}$ . The time-discretisation is then done using a Crank-Nicolson scheme with fixed time step $\Delta t=\frac{1}{50}$ over $T=100$ time units. The resulting nonlinear system at each step is solved with Newton–Raphson line search with residual tolerance of $10^{-10}$ . The implementation uses the FEniCSx software stack (DOLFINx/UFL/Basix) for weak-form assembly and mesh management, with PETSc providing the linear algebra backend and nonlinear solver; see [ Bar+23 ] . Since we are interested in planar fronts, we choose the initial condition $\displaystyle u_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\varepsilon\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{3}A_{\mathrm{hex}}\cos(\mathbf{k}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{x})\Bigr)\frac{1-\tanh(\tfrac{\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{d}-\varphi}{\ell})}{2}$ with a localisation width $\ell=3$ and amplitude $\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{hex}}=\frac{-\beta_{2}-\sqrt{\beta_{2}^{2}-4\mu_{0}(K_{0}+2K_{2})}}{2(K_{0}+2K_{2})},$ which imposes a steep profile in direction $\mathbf{d}=(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))$ connecting hexagons to the spatially homogeneous state. For the choice of parameters, we expect hexagons to be stable with respect to all other stationary patterns: indeed, for ( B.1 ) it holds $K_{0}=-3$ and $K_{2}=-6$ , cf. [ Doe+03 ] , and with $\mu_{0}=1$ we are in the regime before the bifurcation of the mixed-mode branch in Figure 9 . We consider two initial profiles: the first for angle $\theta=0$ and the second for an angle $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ . Note that we shift the second initial condition to the right by setting $\varphi=2\pi$ to make sure no boundary effects from the left-hand boundary affect the dynamics. a $\theta=0$ b $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ Figure 18 : The initial conditions for the finite-element simulations. Note that the envelope of the second initial condition has been shifted to the right by $2\pi$ . The front position $x_{f}(t)$ is then determined by tracking the $L^{2}$ -norm of a rolling $2\pi$ -wide strip in front direction and defining $x_{f}(t)$ to be the rightmost position, where this norm exceeds the threshold $\delta=\frac{0.05}{\sqrt{2}}$ . From the discussion of the selected front speed in Section 1.2 , cf. ( 1.8 ), we obtain the linearly predicted speed $\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{pred}}(\theta)=4\varepsilon\sqrt{(1-(\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{k}_{1})^{2})\mu_{0}},$ where $\mathbf{d}^{\perp}=(-\sin(\theta),\cos(\theta))\perp\mathbf{d}$ . Inserting the parameters gives the predicted front speeds $\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{pred}}(0)=1.2\quad\text{and}\quad c_{\mathrm{pred}}(\tfrac{\pi}{6})\approx 1.039.$ Since we expect a relaxation of the initial condition to the selected front profile and hence a relaxation of the front speed, we fit a linear curve to $x_{f}(t)$ in the interval $[t_{0},t_{1}]$ . Note that we also restrict from above as the plots indicate boundary effects on the right-hand-side boundary to affect the front. For $\theta=0$ , the front speed has relaxed at $t_{0}=20$ and boundary effects are visible after $t_{1}=80$ , while for $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ the front settles at $t_{0}=30$ and boundary effects are visible at $t_{1}=80$ . With this curve, we find the numerical front speeds $\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{num}}(0)\approx 1.1134\quad\text{and}\quad c_{\mathrm{num}}(\tfrac{\pi}{6})=0.9922$ being a reasonable match for the predicted front speed, with a relative error
of approximately $7.2\%$ and $4.5\%$ respectively. The remaining discrepancy is consistent with logarithmic corrections of the position not captured by the leading-order marginal stability prediction, which lead to a correction of the front speed of order $\tfrac{1}{t}$ . These corrections have been rigorously found in monostable fronts in reaction-diffusion systems, see [ AHS25 ] . We also observe that the leading edge of the front exhibits a stripe-like structure before the pattern settles into the hexagonal bulk state, see Figures 20 and 21 . This is consistent with experimental observations, see e.g. [ PN94 , CM99 ] . The data of the front speeds is shown in Figure 19 , and snapshots of the solutions at selected times are shown in Figure 20 . a Front position for $\theta=0$ b Front speed for $\theta=0$ c Front position for $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ d Front speed for $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ Figure 19 : Front position $x_{f}(t)$ , with linear fit $v_{f}$ , and instantaneous front speed $\dot{x}_{f}(t)$ . Note that the relaxation time is visible in the smoothed version of the front speeds. Figure 20 : Snapshots of the solution $u(t,\cdot)$ for $\theta=0$ at times $t=20,50,100$ . The front propagates to the right, leaving a hexagonal
pattern in its wake. Note the stripe-like structure at the leading edge of
the front. Boundary effects near the right-hand boundary are
visible at $t=100$ . Figure 21 : Snapshots of the solution $u(t,\cdot)$ for $\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{6}$ at times $t=20,50,100$ . Note the stripe-like structure at the leading edge of the front, which still carries information about the initial angle. Furthermore, the bulk state becomes unordered over time. Whether this is a genuine effect of the dynamics or an artefact of the boundary condition on top and bottom will remain open. Boundary effects near the right-hand boundary are visible at $t=100$ .
